They are left long on the record and not excused, and the reason is on the batch's face: 176 carries the Sunday's letter, the break with the Registry and the whole of the doorway in two days; 178 carries a paper served in a doorway and a sitting in a room on two days; 175 carries a day of work and the volume's central document at the same hour on the same Friday; 171 carries two days, a new named working person, a refusal posted and a leaf number checked; 180 carries the twenty-fourth wall, two wages' arithmetic and the batch's whole close; 177 carries a wage, a man turned away in a market office and a day at the second seam; 173 carries a wage that did not go in and a man's letter given over on a bench; 179 carries the third column and the tin cup; and 172 carries a man reading his own book for a day. NO SCENE WAS CUT TO SHORTEN ANY CHAPTER AND NO COST WAS DROPPED TO BRING ONE INSIDE A NUMBER.** The hearing room is used **once**, on **Friday the sixteenth of October**. The market wall is used **not at all** in this batch. The market office is used **once**, on **Tuesday the thirteenth of October**, and no two of the three rooms share a day.

**THE SHAPE OF THE BATCH, SAID PLAINLY ONCE: ALL TEN CHAPTERS CLOSE ON THE STANDING INVENTORY AND THERE IS NO ACTION CHAPTER AND NO TRAINING CHAPTER AND NO RECOVERY CHAPTER IN IT.** That is a fact about the shape of the movement, which is about a document and a room and a person not being paid, and in this batch the inventory is not a flourish but the only place a poor yard's whole position is written down at once. **What the volume still owes against `outline/volume-04.md` § *Costs this volume may not waive* — at least one injury to a working crew member with a real cost and a real bill, and one bad day — is unpaid in Movements 1, 2 and 3 and is carried forward.**

**THE MONEY, AND IT CLOSES CHECKABLE ON THE PAGE IN PENCE: A MARK AND TWELVE SHILLINGS AND FOURPENCE in a bag on the morning of Thursday the first of October, and A MARK AND TWELVE SHILLINGS in it on the evening of Tuesday the twentieth. A mark is a hundred and sixty pence and a shilling is twelve, so the whole of the arithmetic in this batch hangs on those two figures: 308p · 304p · 304p · 1,104p · 464p · 944p · 304p.** **THE BATCH CLOSES FOUR PENCE LIGHTER THAN IT OPENED, AND THE FOUR PENCE IS THE POSTAGE OF ONE LETTER IN THE ORDINARY POST, AND THAT IS THE WHOLE OF WHAT THIS YARD HAS SPENT ON ITSELF IN TWENTY DAYS.** The chain: a wage of four on Tuesday the sixth **NOT PAID**, envelope not made, column twenty-one lines and eighty-four marks, **and the number of weeks fallen due with no envelope standing against them is THREE and not two**; a day at the second seam's own face on Friday the ninth, gross seventeen, the yard's **five**, the eight and the share off the top; a wage of four on Tuesday the thirteenth, column twenty-two lines and eighty-eight marks, sixteen envelopes; a day at the same face on Wednesday the fourteenth, gross fifteen, the yard's **three**; a wage of four on Tuesday the twentieth, column **twenty-three lines and ninety-two marks**, seventeen envelopes. **THE WHOLE OF THE MONEY THAT HAS COME INTO THIS YARD SINCE THE FIFTEENTH OF AUGUST IS TWENTY-SIX MARKS; THE WHOLE THAT WENT OUT IN THIS BATCH IS EIGHT MARKS AND FOUR PENCE, BEING TWO WAGES OF FOUR AND ONE LETTER AT FOUR PENCE, AND NOTHING ELSE WENT OUT OF THE BAG AT ALL.**

**THE READ-OUT DEVICE WAS NOT USED IN THIS BATCH AND ITS ONE USE PER BATCH IS CARRIED FORWARD. THE READ-BACK — the ship's hand reading the day's entries back from the top of the bench and the holder reading them back after her, nobody else in the shed — RUNS IN FOUR OF THE TEN CHAPTERS, 172, 178, 179 AND 180, and not in 173, 175, 176 or 177, and is not a use of the device and may be used on any night without spending anything. THE FIGURE OF SEVEN THAT THIS FILE CARRIED BEFORE THE REVIEW REPAIRS IS WITHDRAWN AND NAMED AS WITHDRAWN; the method is a plain string search for *read back* and *read-back* across the ten chapters and it returns four. A three-pass duplication audit run after the repairs — byte comparison, a forty-word opening fingerprint and pairwise containment, then sentences — returns ZERO byte-identical paragraphs in 725 compared, ZERO forty-word opening matches inside the batch, ZERO containment pairs inside the batch, and 23 repeated sentences of eight words or more inside the batch, every one of them this yard's standing inventory or the wage-column line its own bookkeeping repeats.** A previous audit of this batch reported *354 paragraphs, zero duplicates* and missed a truncated copy of thirty words at `chapter-0176.md:41`, which a byte-comparison cannot see; the three-pass method and its results are at `state/continuity.md` § 10, and the one duplicated sentence the new method found inside the batch — a woman of thirty stating the first of her four grounds in identical words at a copying counter and in a doorway — was varied in the second instance, with no fact added and no fact removed. The full result and the reason the rest are not repaired are at `state/continuity.md` § 10 and the repairs themselves at § *Batch 0003 (Volume 04) review repairs*.

### Chapter 171 — A Rota Day, and a Woman With a Counter Off the Fish Market

Thursday the first and Friday the second of October, on the mole's crown, in a doorway, and in the shed. **The cistern behind the engine house was charged at no figure by strangers and this party did not walk it, and that is the first rota day of this month and the fifteenth since the third of August, and this party has walked none of the fifteen.** Alden Marr is at the far bench on both days and is not asked for anything on either. **THE PLATE NOTE STANDS OPEN, AND THE PARTY ENTERS AGAIN THAT WHATEVER CLOSES IT WILL CLOSE IT IN AN OFFICE AND NOT IN A SHED AND THAT IT DOES NOT KNOW WHAT THAT IS.** Then, at about eleven on the Thursday morning, **Pernel Saye, fifty-eight, of this harbor, a copyist, with a counter in a room off the fish market that is open to anybody, where a sheet is a penny and forty sheets a shilling, comes up the hill unasked and at her own cost with a board of papers and asks a man who reads a page for one thing only.** The head of her own sheet says: *Job 4 — Fri. 8 Sept — Morrow circuit office, copying room. From the file of the ASTER GANNET. Leaf 62. One leaf, copied entire, eleven sheets, returned with the file.* **The yard reads those four lines twice and does not read the rest of it and does not ask to, and the number sixty-two goes into the first column in the holder's own left hand with the date against it and her name on the line and a reason against the line, and the reason is that the party cannot check the number, cannot see the file, has not asked for the file and is not going to, and that she has been up those stairs twice in her life and is not going a third time, which anybody may come up this hill and ask her.** And Calder Marr says the thing that makes this the batch's hinge: **he has got a book of his own in a file in a city that cannot be got out of it, and he has not got that file and is not going to ask for it, and there is nobody in this town who can get it for him and if there were he could not pay her.** And on the Friday a **letter went into the letterbox for FOUR PENCE, paid at a counter in the holder's own hand**, restating four working reasons for refusing a man of the first lay the figure of the bar of gravel, and Nessa Pike says the shape of that out loud and is heard, and he posts it, and the whole of his reason is that a man refused in a doorway is going to say so in a market whether the yard writes to him or not, and the third time this yard has published something it has cost four pence and he does not know what the fourth will cost and is not going to find out by not posting a letter.

### Chapter 172 — A Man Reading His Own Book

Saturday the third and Sunday the fourth of October, in the shed. **A man of this yard sat down on Saturday morning with his own crew's book and read it, and it took him until the light went, and nobody had ever asked him to and he had never done it.** Forty-seven weeks, two ruled columns and a third at the head of a page in a boy's hand, written in three hands. He reads the first column all day and knows what every line of it means. **At about half past four in the afternoon he gets to the fourteenth day of August and stops, because he can bring up the first four words of a line he wrote in the second column at about nine that evening and cannot bring up the rest, and the four words are *a man in a boat*, and the rest of it is in his own left hand.** He can say exactly what he did that day on the face of the second seam. He cannot remember writing the line. **Nessa Pike will not read it out to him, and gives the reason: that a person reading a man's second column to him at his own table to be kind is how a column stops being a column and becomes the person who was in the room.** And on the Sunday night he enters that he had decided on the Saturday to tell Mara Venn that he wrote down in the second column on the eighth of September a sentence she told him on the seventh of August not to write down — **and that he did not tell her, and that the reason is not a reason against the telling and is this: that on Saturday he found out he could not remember one line of his own second column, and a man who has found that out is not a man who is going to be able to tell a woman on a mole's steps that he kept something back from her for six weeks and be believed about it.** And she says **you are not going to ask her to be the woman who checks your page.** And the first column carries the Alden days, the third column not filled, the far bench empty on both days for the first time in a month, and the standing decision not to go to the harbor authority for anything, with the ninth acknowledgement named as the reason.

### Chapter 173 — The Twenty-Second Wall, and a Wage That Does Not Go In

Monday the fifth and Tuesday the sixth of October, at the mole's steps, at the head of the dock, and in the shed. **The wall behind the reeve's bench came down bare at eight and that was twenty-two Mondays since the first of May, and the clerk kept his own page and did not mention it, and the cistern was charged by strangers at no figure and this party did not walk it.** And on the Tuesday **the wage of four marks did not go in and the envelope was not made, the third Tuesday in a row, and the number of weeks fallen due this year with no envelope standing against them is now THREE — the fifteenth of August, the fifth of September and the sixth of October — entered in the same breath because a yard which is asked how many weeks it has paid has to say all three at once rather than the one that is easier.** And at about half past two on the Tuesday afternoon **Wil Tranter comes up the hill with a letter he wrote to a surveyor in a city on Sunday night and did not send to this party, and says so first, and reads it out.** The letter refuses to set his word under her finding and gives the reason in full: he did not make the figure, and a man's name under a line about water is the only thing a man of the first lay has that a man of no lay does not have, and he is fifty-one and has a boat and four men. **He says the yard's letter of the second instant reached him in his own wheelhouse on Monday morning and that he read it in four lines in the holder's own hand and did not know what to do with it for four days.** And he says the whole of what it costs: **that a man with a page cannot act on a name that will not go on a page, and that this party has a document from him and cannot put it in front of anybody, and that a copy of a record is a thing nobody in this harbor can check.** And he is not thanked, is paid nothing, is offered nothing, and says out loud that he would rather it was on the page that the yard offered and he would not have it, because in about four years a man in a market is going to say the yard sent a man of the first lay to a city with a question, and both halves are going to be true and only one is going to be the whole of it.

### Chapter 174 — A Day That Was There to Be Booked and Was Not

Wednesday the seventh and Thursday the eighth of October, in the shed, in the market office, and at the head of the dock. **A day of work on the second seam's own face was there to be booked on the Wednesday and was not booked, and the reason is a fact about this harbor and not about this yard: the third lay is short and has been since August, the tide gang is four men short, one herring berth of two is out, and her vessel has a started plank.** Calder Marr will not enter it as *no work found* — **that would be a lie and a flattering one and this column is not the place for either** — and he takes the Friday instead at her figure, and she says she would have taken the Wednesday if she had had it. And on the Thursday, a rota day, the cistern is charged by strangers and this party does not walk it, and **the holder goes down to the market office, buys a penny's worth of nothing, is in a room where eleven people are not talking to him, and asks the press-keeper for nothing** — and she tells him, unasked, that she has set about nine hundred sheets since the fifteenth of August, has never been asked what is on one and has not read one, and has been asked three times this month by men with money to bring the slab forward and has not. **Then the plate note, for about forty minutes, and it does not move: the party entered on the eighth, against itself and with the hour against it, that it does not know whether the removal of a leaf from a file in a room on a second floor of this town is the same thing as the change of politics in an office it has been waiting for since the seventeenth of January, and that it is not going to guess, and that a number in a book is not a change of politics in an office and is not going to be made into one by anybody in that shed.**

### Chapter 175 — Five Marks, and a Counterfoil in a Box Under a Counter

Friday the ninth and Saturday the tenth of October, on the second seam's own face, at the mole's steps, in a room off the fish market, and in the shed. **The yard worked about four hours and forty minutes on the face of the second seam with nobody in the water and a hand lead's line over the side and back four times, and gross on the board was seventeen and the yard's was five, and the five went up the mole's steps in daylight in front of about eleven people and no figure was argued.** And at about half past ten the same morning **Mara Venn, thirty, of no office and of no party, sat down at a copying counter in a room off the fish market and asked for a fair copy of her own November paper at a penny a sheet, and then said the rest of it before it was asked for.** Pernel Saye put her pen down — which she does not do in the middle of a morning — went down and came up with a grey card about the size of a man's hand, and put it on the counter flat and turned it round. **THE CARD IS THE BATCH'S CENTRAL DOCUMENT:** *Morrow circuit office — copying room. Second day of September. Withdrawn: file of the ASTER GANNET, leaf 62. One leaf, to the copying room on the eighth instant, copied entire, returned with the file. On the application of a commissioned officer of the Crown Navy, by letter of the first instant, a copy of which letter is on the file. This office agrees to the withdrawal. **Reason entered by this office: risk management.** The leaf is not to be produced. A copy is held.* **And then, in her own trade's words, THE WHOLE OF THE MOVEMENT'S SECOND HALF: "That is a form, and the form is the thing. A page is a thing a person wrote. A form is a thing an office wrote about the page. And when the office writes the form, the form goes in front of the page, because that is what forms are for, and a man in a room in October who is handed the form will read the form. He will not turn it over."** And then the second half of it: the foot of the copyist's job sheet in her own hand says **the copy of leaf 62 is to be read with leaf 88**, and Mara has never seen leaf 88, and leaf 62 is her own refusal of the eleventh of November on four grounds with her trade against her name and the office's stamp on it because she would not let it leave without one — **and the first of the four is that the office will not countersign an instrument that puts a person inside a closure that the same office's own notice created.** And her last line, which is the whole of her position: **"I have got a number that two people in this town can check and neither of us has seen the page. I have got a counterfoil in a box under your counter that is going to be waste paper in four years. I have got my own copy of a page that is in a city. And I have got a slip that says the copy of my page is to be read with a page I have never seen. Three things that all agree with each other and not one of them is the page."** And she pays the penny a sheet and says she has not done a thing at nothing since the fourth of August. And on the Saturday a man of the third lay offers a wharf day for the Monday and **is refused, and the whole of the reason is said out loud and it is a bad one and it is a working one, and he says he has had a Monday out of a month this year as well.**

### Chapter 176 — A Letter With a Leaf Number On It, and a Woman Who Came Up the Hill

Sunday the eleventh and Monday the twelfth of October, in a room off the fish market, at the mole's steps, and in the yard. **On the Sunday a woman of thirty sits at her own table, reads her own writing of the eleventh of November last, and cannot remember the fourth of the four grounds she wrote, and has to look; and she writes that down unsigned, with the reason she has never signed anything since the seventh of April.** Then **the break with the Registry, in her own hand, in four lines, in the ordinary post at half past two, at a penny out of her own pocket: that she will not be cited, quoted, produced, or asked again by that office or by any office of it; that no form, sheet, notation, hand or date of hers is to be used to support an account she did not write and has not read; and that she is not resigning anything, because there is nothing to resign from, and that she has been waiting eleven months to find out what to call the thing and it turns out that it has a name and the name is a form.** And on the Monday **the wall came down bare for the twenty-third time, and the cistern was charged by strangers, and Alden Marr is at the far bench.** And at about four in the afternoon **Mara Venn comes up the hill and sits down in the doorway, and she was not asked and nobody sent her, and the party had not known she was in the harbor**, and she says the three things out loud before she starts: that she is going to say it once in about nine minutes, that she is not going to answer a question, and that she is not going to be helped with anything. And she tells the whole of the card, and the four grounds, and the closure — and then **THE SPINE OF THE BATCH, TWICE OVER: "I am not going to give you the line. On the twenty-ninth of September I priced you a line in my own trade's words with my name and my price on it, and I said no, and the price was not six marks and two days and it was not money, it was an undertaking in your own first column in your own left hand that this party does not ask me for anything again. And the price has not changed. A file has come to us. That is not a reason and it is not a hardship."** And: **"if I wrote that line out again on this bench this afternoon in this yard's own book, in my own hand, with my name and my trade and a date on it, and you had it a week before the sitting, it would be worth precisely nothing and you would know it and so would I, and the reason is that a line of mine in a book of yours is a document, and the Registry's page is a document, and a man in a room in October holds two documents and they are the same instrument and only one of them has a seal on it, and the one without the seal is mine. That is how a person of no office becomes a thing a person with an office can use."** And he does not thank her and does not say he is sorry and does not ask her one question and says why: **a party which has been refused once and then comes up a hill with a second errand is a party which has decided that a person is a door.** And she says: **good, and I am not going to ask either of you for anything, and I want that on your page, because in about two years one of you is going to want something.** And she tells them, last and out loud, that **the first of her four grounds is about a plate in eleven feet in this mole, and that she came up this hill four days in advance of either of them wanting to ask her, and that neither of them is going to ask.** And Nessa Pike says the sentence the batch closes on: **"This yard has a number in its first column off a copying job sheet. It has a woman in a room off the fish market it cannot pay. It has a file on a second floor it has not asked for and is not going to. And it is going into a room on Friday morning with a book, and it is not represented, and it is going to be told what it means. Four things and not one of them can be put in front of anybody."** And **he did not tell her about the seventh of August, and the second column says so with the reason and the date, and the reason is the reason of the fourth and there is nothing new to add to it and he is not going to invent a second one.**

### Chapter 177 — A Wage That Goes In, and a Man Who Came Up the Hill Asking the Wrong Yard

Tuesday the thirteenth and Wednesday the fourteenth of October, at the head of the dock, in the market office, in the yard, and in the shed. **The wage of four marks goes in at about eleven in the morning into an envelope in the holder's own left hand with the week on the outside, and the column is twenty-two lines and eighty-eight marks, sixteen envelopes on the bench, three weeks fallen due, and about nine people in the street say nothing at all about the third in a row, which is what about nine people say about a wage in a hard month.** And at about ten that morning **in the market office, which is open to anybody, a man of the first lay named Oswin Rede, about fifty, comes in with a letter from a city and asks the room, in about nine people's hearing, what a form says, and there is a person in this room on a Tuesday and a Friday morning who would know and she was not in it on the said morning, and had said at the counter that she is not coming on Tuesdays or Fridays any more, that the reason is her own and is not going to be given, and that anybody who wants it can ask her and she will not answer that either — and nobody in that room could tell him anything, and about nine people heard a man say out loud that a thing he had paid for was being explained to a city by a woman he had never met and could not pay, and nothing whatever was done about it.** And on the Wednesday **he comes up the hill with the letter anyway, and is told the whole of it in about nine sentences: that the form is not this yard's and is not in this yard's book and is not in this yard's hand, that the woman is not on this crew and is owed forty marks first after food since the fifteenth of October, and that this party is not going to tell him where she is and will say why, and the why is about her and not about him.** And he is sent down the hill and says he is not owed anything by this yard and that nobody in it has asked him for a thing and that he is not going to be a man who was helped. And the yard works about four hours and fifty minutes on the second seam's own face and the board is fifteen and the yard's is three, and the eight and the share come off the top.

### Chapter 178 — A Paper Served in a Doorway, and a Sitting at Ten in the Morning

Thursday the fifteenth and Friday the sixteenth of October, in a doorway, and in the hearing room over the tide-gang store on the west basin. **Ambrose Sell, clerk of the circuit office and clerk of that sitting, serves a paper of six paragraphs in the doorway of the shed at about eleven in the morning in front of three people, and says his name and the name of his office before he reads it.** The paper appoints the sitting for **Friday the sixteenth instant at ten in the morning**; requires the four parties to attend; requires the six things of the fourth paragraph of the notice of the tenth again and says that producing them is not a concession and is not an admission; says a schedule is read into the record and no copy is given to any party; **states that the registry file of the ASTER GANNET is before the inquiry, that one leaf is called for and is not produced, and that the reason of the non-production is on a form of the Registry's own dated the second of September which is not before the sitting and is not to be produced to the party**; and says nothing touches the second season. **And the magistrate's name is on the original and not on the copy: Halven Rook, magistrate of this circuit, who has not been in this harbor and is not going to be, and the clerk says the truth about it, which is that it is on a paper and not in a room.** And he says two things that are not on the paper: that he has read what the Registry's form says and will not say it **and will not say that he has read it**, because in about four years a man in a city is going to be able to say that a clerk of this circuit knew what a page said and could not deny it; and that the party is not represented and he is going to have to do that again in about forty minutes. And on the Friday **the wall behind the reeve's bench is bare and has been since Monday the twelfth, and about nine people are in the room, and the party is not represented, and it produces the six things whole and unshortened in about eleven minutes, and NOT ONE LINE OF THE CREW'S BOOK IS READ ALOUD, and no copy of any record is made in that room.** Then **the clerk reads the schedule into the record in about four minutes, and a list is a list: the file of the ASTER GANNET fourth on it, leaf 62 called for, leaf 62 not produced, the reason on a Registry form of the second of September, the form not produced, and the two words are not in the schedule and are not read out in that room.** And at about twenty minutes to eleven **the clerk asks one question, and it is the batch's cost: *is there any person in this harbor whose hand is on a page of that file and who is not an officer of any office in it* — and Calder Marr stands up in front of about nine people and says yes, and that she is not on this crew and is not of this yard and is not a holder and is not on the four names, and that he is not going to give her name and is not going to say that she is in this harbor, and that he has entered both with reasons, and that he wants two things said: that there is a person, because there is, and that a party which published itself in July is now going to have a line in a record in a city saying there is a woman in this harbor the yard of Morrow Quay will not name.** And the clerk writes it down and reads it back and enters that too. And nobody asked about the second column, nobody asked about the third, nobody asked the question the holder has had in his own second column since the tenth of September, and nobody mentioned the word.

### Chapter 179 — A Tin Cup on the End of a Bench, and a Column Nobody Fills

Saturday the seventeenth and Sunday the eighteenth of October, in the shed, and on the mole's crown. **Nothing happened on the Saturday and the party entered that, and the reason against the line is that a day on which nothing happens still happened and the only way it gets into a book that can be read in a room in a fortnight is by somebody writing it down on the day.** A man of the first lay of about ninety tons asks about a thing on his stem and is told that a man of this yard does not turn a thing over for nothing and is not taking it on in October, and says he had not expected it in October and will come again in the spring. A man of the third lay says in passing that **the cistern was charged on the Thursday and not on the Monday**, and the party enters that it did not know that and does not know it now. And at about half past four in the afternoon **Nessa Pike says the thing she has been carrying since Monday: she could fill the third column today, she has got nine sentences, they are what a woman of thirty said on the bench on Monday the twelfth in about nine minutes in about six people's hearing, and she is not going to write them — because the third column is a page where a man wrote down what he said when he was not in the boat, and that is the only reason it has ever been worth anything, and if those nine sentences go in it then this yard is holding a page about what a woman said to it in a doorway, and in about four years there is going to be a page in a city that says what a yard of Morrow Quay knew in October, and anybody can read it.** And then the second thing, **and it is the spine moving again and it moves because of her: "You are going to tell me or you are not. I am not going to ask you. I am not going to ask you tonight and I am not going to ask you in a market and I am not going to ask you in a room in a city in four years, and I want it on your page tonight in your own hand that you had a week to ask me and that you did not."** He writes it down. And on the Sunday at about eleven in the morning **he puts a tin cup of water on the end of the far bench and goes back to his own bench and says nothing; the man does not turn round and does not drink it and does not refuse it and says no word, and the cup is still there on Monday morning at nine and is not there at four in the afternoon, and neither brother saw who took it and neither brother has said a word about it.** And at about four he says out loud that he has done that once and is not going to do it again, and why: **that a cup of water on the end of a bench is a question in a language he made up, and he does not get to make up a language for him.**

### Chapter 180 — The Twenty-Fourth Wall, and a Reason Against the Line

Monday the nineteenth and Tuesday the twentieth of October, at the mole's steps, at the head of the dock, and in the shed. **The wall came down bare at eight and that was twenty-four Mondays since the first of May, and the clerk kept his own page and did not mention it, and a sitting was held in that room on the sixteenth and nothing has been put on that board since.** The cistern is charged by strangers — the sixth rota day of the month and **the nineteenth since the third of August, walked on none of them**. The tin cup is on the far bench at nine and is not on it at four. A man of the third lay comes back about a wharf day and **it is booked for Friday the twenty-third and the figure is not entered because the board is not written until the day, and a figure written on a note is not a figure paid until somebody puts the money on the counter.** And on the Tuesday the wage of four goes in at about eleven, the column is **twenty-three lines and ninety-two marks**, seventeen envelopes, three weeks fallen due. And at about half past nine on the Tuesday night in the shed **the whole of the month is said out loud once, in about four minutes, and it is in the book, and it is the five things the party can do about it, and four of them cost money it has not got**: go to the second floor and ask for the page, which is a request to an office of a third party about a document in that office's own file, and it would be asking a woman of fifty-eight to go up a third time; write to a city, which this party has not done since the third of May; go to the harbor authority and ask for a tenth thing in that ledger, and the ninth is the last and a tenth may not be entered; go to the woman, whose price on the twenty-ninth of September has not moved; **and be in a room and say it — and the party was in a room on Friday and said a different thing, and the thing it said is in a record in a city now and it says there is a woman in this harbor the yard will not name.** And Nessa Pike takes the fifth apart: **"A wall has nothing in it you can be made to answer for. A room has a person in it and a clerk with a pen, and a thing you said forty minutes after you have said it."** And Calder says the other half, and the shape of that sentence is said once, about something new, and nobody in that shed remarks on it and neither of them improves on it: **a hearing is a room this party can stand in for nothing, and what comes out of it goes on a page in a city and cannot be got off, and the room is open and the page is not, and he has been using one of those to talk himself out of the other for five months.** And the first column carries the wage, the envelopes, the three weeks, the pence, the booked day, and **the entry that the reading of this yard's own book in a room HAS NEVER HAPPENED, did not happen on the sixteenth, is coming, and the week of it is not in this party's keeping and is not going to be.** And the second column carries the month's whole account, the four reasons and one figure, and the **entry of the days Alden Marr was at the far bench — the second, the fifth, the eighth, the twelfth, the eighteenth and the nineteenth, being six days in twenty — and neither brother went over and neither brother has said why.** And the last line of the batch: **a working harbor is poorer on the last page of this month than it was on the first page of it, and a yard of two and a half people is poorer with a mark and twelve shillings in a bag than it was with a mark and twelve shillings and fourpence, and both of those are this yard's own figures, and a party that has found out what an office says about itself is not a yard that has found out a thing it can do, and this party has not found out a thing it can do, and it is going to be in a room about the middle of November, and it does not know when.**
