### Chapter 191 — One Sheet in the Holder's Own Hand, and an Envelope Not Made
Monday the sixteenth and Tuesday the seventeenth of November, at the mole's steps, and in the shed. **The slate wall comes down bare for the twenty-eighth Monday since the first of May and the clerk keeps his own page and does not mention it. The cistern is charged at no figure on the rota day and this party walks it, and the walk is nil. Alden Marr is at the far bench on the Monday from ten until about four, not asked, asking nothing, and Calder does not go over.** In the shed at half past eight the holder writes out one sheet, in his own left hand, dated: an application to the circuit office of this place for a copy of one record of that office, being the record of the entry of one item of recovered transparent mineral in the custody room of that office, which says on its face that it asks for a copy of a record and not for the object, that a copy of a record is a thing nobody in this harbor can check, that a party may apply at its own cost and no date attaches, and that the release of such a copy is not in this yard's keeping and has never been in it, and that the party is not saying no to it in four years and is not saying yes either. **It is not signed by two hands, and the reason is given.** A penny of the ordinary post goes on it at about half past two and the bag is a mark and six shillings and a penny after it, which is 261p. **On the Tuesday the wage of four marks does not go in, no envelope is made, and the number of weeks fallen due with no envelope standing against them is now FIVE, all five given in the same breath, and the bag was 379p short of the wage.** The reason it applies now, when it entered in June and again on the eighteenth of September that it would not, is in the second column with a reason against the line: not that the old reason has gone soft, but that a copy of a record carries on its own face the limits the record itself carries, and a limit printed on the face of a sheet is the one kind of limit that does not arrive in a city as a bare line with nothing behind it. **And on the same night a sixth occasion not to tell her is entered, on a line of its own, with the reason of the fourth of October given again and the undertaking not to invent a second one.**

### Chapter 192 — Three Instruments on a Bench, and a Room That Is Not Free
Wednesday the eighteenth and Thursday the nineteenth of November, in the shed, and on the mole's steps. **No day of work is booked on either day and the board is not written and nobody comes up the hill with a day in his mouth, and the reason against the line is that a day on which nothing happens still happened.** In the shed at ten the ship's hand puts three things on a bench: a page in a shed, worth what two men in one room are worth; a page in a market, read by about four hundred people and taken down by any man with a knife; and a page in a room in a city, which goes on a record and cannot be got off. **She says the market is the only one of the three that arrives with its reasons on it, and that she is not going to let this yard have it, and the reason is that four people of this coast wrote a line and signed it and a reason on a market wall is a reason in a market; and the holder says then we do not put it on a wall, and she says yes and gives the reason; and she says she is for the room and wants it written down that she knows what the room costs.** On the Thursday, on the rota day, the cistern is charged at no figure and walked on none, and **Alden Marr is at the far bench and was not at it on the Wednesday at any hour.** At about eleven, in the open air at the foot of the mole's steps in about eleven people's hearing, the holder asks the officer of the harbor authority for **a room and a day and not an instrument**, saying first that there are eight acknowledgements and the ninth is spent and a tenth may not be entered and was not asked for, and that he is not asking that office to seat anybody, **because the power to seat one of two disputants belongs to the inquiry and not to the writ and the two are not the same instrument.** Kest names the price before he says yes: the room is over the tide-gang store, a sitting in it shuts the store, and **one man of the tide gang loses his Thursday morning, and the tide gang is four men short and will be four men short at the first of March, and he will not name him because the moment he names him he is a man in this business.** Then three more things, and the price of the clerk's day falls on his own office and not on the party, and **he says he is not a friend of this party and has never said he was.**

### Chapter 193 — Four Marks on a Board, and Two Shillings on a Counter
Friday the twentieth and Saturday the twenty-first of November, on the face of the second seam, at a bonesetter's room off the market, and in the shed. **Gross on the board thirteen. The eighth, one. The share, one. The victuals, one. A hired hand, three. A second hired hand, three. Nine of thirteen paid at the mole and the yard's being four**, and the strapping comes off on the Saturday and not on the Friday and everybody in that yard knows it and the hirer knew before he came and did not ask. **The ship's hand keeps the book and the count and the times and hauls the line with her right hand and does not take a pump handle, and the hirer says he asked for a pump and not for a party, and a party of one and a half is a party of one.** The cost is entered: a day that goes slower is a day not earned whole. On the Saturday a strapping comes off at a bonesetter's room at a figure of **two shillings**, paid into her own hand at her own counter, and she says why it is not the figure of the twenty-fourth of October, and that a visit on the fourteenth day is a look and not a visit, and **that the bone has knitted straight and the wrist does not turn all the way round in a cold month for two years and the only thing that makes it worse is a boat on a cold tide with a fall in the hand, and that she does not want it in her own book.** And the ship's hand enters in her own hand that until the first of March this yard is a party of one and a half and not two, and that every day this yard books in this month is a day it books with a man in it.

### Chapter 194 — A List of What Is Not Going Into the Room, and a Form With an Office's Name on It
Sunday the twenty-second and Monday the twenty-third of November, in the shed, and on the mole's steps. **On the Sunday the ship's hand writes a list of eleven things that are not going into the room and it is read out in the shed and the holder writes it into the first column in his own left hand and neither of them adds to it nor takes anything off it.** The eleven are the place of the case of five bells, the four people it belongs to, the limit they wrote, the plate at the fifth seam, the two objects on the crown, the boy, the forty marks to him, the forty marks to a person of no office, the delay, a letter expected in the post, and the two figures for the bar of gravel with the Kettle. **The holder stops her at the ninth item and makes a tenth of it, which is the letter, and the reason is that a line about a person of no office in a room is a document about her.** On the Monday the wall comes down bare for the twenty-ninth Monday, and the cistern is charged on the rota day and walked on none. **In the afternoon Ambrose Sell comes up the hill with a form folded inside his coat and not in his hand and says that no date attaches, that a party may apply and that the application grants nothing and makes nothing, that a copy will be made at a counter and will carry on its face the limits the record itself carries in the record's own words, and that at the foot where a signature would be there is the name of an office and no man's name, because a clerk's name would let a man in a city say in four years that a clerk decided a copy should be made, and that is not what happened.** He reads the eleven lines back twice and says he has not asked about any of the eleven.

### Chapter 195 — The Face of a Copy, and Two Different Lengths
Tuesday the twenty-fourth and Wednesday the twenty-fifth of November, at the mole's steps, on the counter of the circuit office and back up the hill, and in the shed. **The wage of four marks is PAID into an envelope in the holder's own left hand with the week on the outside of it, in daylight, in front of about nine people; the column goes to twenty-six lines and a hundred and four marks and the envelopes on the bench to twenty, and five weeks remain fallen due with no envelope against them.** In the afternoon the holder goes down to the counter of that office's copying room and comes back up with one sheet, and a clerk gives his name and the name of his office and says three things about a thing leaving a counter, the third of which is that after the first of December nobody in this harbor will ask again whether this yard wanted it read in a room. On the Wednesday **the sheet is read out whole in the shed by the ship's hand and read back by the holder and nobody else is in the room, and it is six heads and about a page and a half: what it is, the item with no number on it, how it came here, the limits in the record's own words, the discrepancy — a line of a manifest altered, bearing a date, not showing by whose hand, at whose instance or with what intent, naming no person — and what it does not show.** She says: on the face of it a page and a half, and the yard can check none of it, and those are two different lengths and they were before anybody in that room had read a word. He enters that neither half of the manifest finding is a discovery in this yard's book, that the July sentence is not improved by asking for one, and **that from the twenty-fifth of November this yard is a party that has seen a document and said nothing about it in a market and there is no version of this yard after today in which that is not a fact about it.** **Alden Marr is at the far bench on the Wednesday and was not at it on the Tuesday at any hour.**

### Chapter 196 — A Page and a Half, and a Thing in a Room in This Town
Thursday the twenty-sixth and Friday the twenty-seventh of November, in the hearing room over the tide-gang store on the west basin. **THE VOLUME'S CLIMAX ROOM. The cistern is charged on the rota day and walked on none, and the store is shut behind them and the men who count the catch in it are in another building, and one of them is a morning down, and he is not named in the book and this party does not know his name.** At ten on the Thursday, about eleven people in the room, a clerk enters the date and the hour and the place before he says anything, and puts on the first line of the record that the office is not sitting to decide anything, finds against nobody, and has not convicted this yard and is not going to, and gives the reading to the ship's hand and not the man who brought the sheet up the hill, **because in October a man read a book in that room and gave up a column of it.** The copy is read out whole, head by head, and she reads the second head twice because four people at the back had not had it, and says the third head is a check on this yard and not on that office, and reads the sixth head last and slowest because it is the head about nothing at all. **The clerk enters that the answer came from the ship's hand, which is the second time that has been entered in that room.** Then the question: does the party apply for the item. **The answer is no, in about four minutes, and the clerk enters that the item stays in the custody of that office and that this party accepts that in that room and is not going to be told it was sorry about it in a street** — and the holder says in that room that if it came up this hill it would be worth more than every mark that has gone through this yard's hands since the first of May and that he would not know what to do with it, and that four people of this coast wrote a line in October, and that this yard is not going to use a piece of glass to be the reason it does not ask. **The shape of sentence is said once, in that room, and improved on by nobody.** On the Friday, about nine people, the clerk asks, in his own words and having written the question in his own page first, whether the holder can say what was in the water off the south end of this mole on any day this year — **and he says he can give a figure, an hour, a state of the tide and the name of the man who hauled the line, and that he cannot tell the room what was under that water and never could, and that he said a thing in a market on the eighteenth of July that was wrong in three parts out of four and has not taken it back, and that this yard is not going to ask for two rooms, and that he cannot vouch for the water with his own ears, and that he is not going to get out of it by being right about something.** The clerk writes it all and reads it back and nobody in the room says a single thing.

### Chapter 197 — One Sheet in Her Hand, and an Office Saying a Thing About Itself
Saturday the twenty-eighth and Sunday the twenty-ninth of November, in the shed. **No day of work is booked and the board is not written, and the reason is that a day on which nothing happens still happened, and which is a day on which two things happened and neither of them was work.** On the Saturday one sheet comes up the hill in the ordinary post, thin, folded once with the fold gone soft, dated the twenty-seventh, and the ship's hand reads it out standing up and then a second time. **It is her answer, price first and answer second: the price is that the letter be kept, that what she has written go in the second column with a reason against the line, and that not one word of it go in a market, and her reason is that in about four years a man in a city is going to read that a yard of Morrow Quay went to a woman it owed forty marks to and asked whether it might pay her part of it and was refused, and that the said reading is going to be true of a man like him and is not going to be true of him, and that there is nothing she can write from where she sits that will make the difference. THE ANSWER IS NO, because a part payment is a receipt and a receipt is a document and a document is how a person of no office becomes a thing a person with an office can use. The forty marks stands first after food and has not moved. The six marks and two days has not moved and is not going to be reduced by anybody including by her, because it is her price and not a debt and this is the first time anybody has tried to make them one. Do not ask me again this winter, the reason being that a price is a price and he asked for one.** She says she is not going to be thanked and not going to be told he is sorry, and that she read it twice because a crease about four inches down and she was not going to lose a word to a crease, and she asks that the one thing written in the first column be that the whole of the sheet is that the answer is no and that not one word of it is about the copy or the room. **On the Sunday a second sheet comes up the hill: the circuit office's acknowledgment of the discrepancy, half a sheet, no signature, an office's name at the foot, which says a line of a manifest was altered and that the office does not state by whose hand, at whose instance or with what intent, and which gives its reason on its face — a copy which has been made cannot be unmade, and an office which has released a transcript containing a discrepancy and has not acknowledged it will be read in four years as an office which released it — and which finds against nobody and does not alter the record.** The ship's hand says a copy is a penny a sheet and this yard is not going to pay a penny a sheet on about four hundred people and is not going to ask a copyist to make one for nothing and is not going to go to that counter and say it is for the yard, and that it will be read in a market by whoever pays a penny for a sheet of it.

### Chapter 198 — A Penny a Sheet, and a Wage Not Paid
Monday the thirtieth of November and Tuesday the first of December, at the fish market, at the mole's steps, and in the shed. **The wall comes down bare for the thirtieth Monday since the first of May and there has not been a sitting in that room since the twenty-seventh, and the cistern is charged on the rota day and walked on none, and the party is not going to say what that and the charge of the ninth of November make between them.** In the market on the Monday morning a man of the third lay is telling two other men about a sheet that came up the hill on Sunday, and **Pernel Saye, fifty-eight, who keeps a copying counter in a room off the fish market open to anybody from eight until the market is off the boards, where a sheet is a penny and forty sheets a shilling, comes over and says that a sheet of that office is a public sheet and is the only kind worth the candle, that in thirty-one years of copying she has never copied one of that office's for nothing and is not going to start, that she will copy it at a penny a sheet and read it out herself because she knows which forms have a reason in the middle of them and that this one has and it is in the fourth paragraph, and that she is not doing it for that yard and that the yard is not to come up to her counter and say it is for them.** She makes twenty-one copies at about nine minutes a sheet. **On the Tuesday the wage of four marks does not go in, no envelope is made, and the number of weeks fallen due is now SIX, all six given in the same breath, and the bag was 403p short of the wage.** At about a quarter past eleven, at the head of the fish dock, in about forty people's hearing, **she reads the whole form out in about nine minutes with twenty-one copies on a board at a penny a sheet and says first that it is not that yard's sheet, and reads the fourth paragraph slowly and says that is the part they put in on purpose and that there is not one office on this coast that has ever done that in writing, and says at the end that a thing read twice in a market is a thing made to sound better than it is, and that she is not going to be thanked by that yard and would rather they did not come and stand by her.** A man of the first lay buys four copies at four pence. Two men of the third lay read it standing up and give it back without buying a sheet. **The yard reads nothing out on that day and the entry says on its face that this was not a reading of its own columns.**

### Chapter 199 — Two Questions in a Market, and a Note That Is Not Re-Written
Wednesday the second and Thursday the third of December, at the fish market, at the head of this dock, and in the shed. **In the market, with about forty people in it, Wil Tranter, fifty-one, of the first lay, master of a coaster of about a hundred and ninety tons, says at the start that he is the one asking and that what he did in October and what he is doing now are not the same, and asks which office agreed to an alteration of a line of a manifest, and says he is not asking whose hand wrote it and not asking who ordered it.** The holder refuses in about four minutes and gives the whole reason: **the reason is a woman, not her name and not that she is in this harbor, and the only person on this coast who can tell a man what a form of that office says is a woman of no office four days up the coast, and this party has written to her once in four months and is not going up the hill with a second errand, because a party which has been refused once and then comes back with a second errand is a party which has decided that a person is a door; and the other half is that this yard entered on the sixth of May that it does not put a name on a thing it cannot check, and a man of the first lay asked for a name at a wall on the fifth of November and was refused in about four minutes.** The man says that is a no with a reason in it and that it is the first no anybody in that market has heard that was not dressed up as a principle, and that he will take it. **Then he asks the second thing, and it is the new-volume question: what an office that has kept records for three hundred years thought it was guarding against at the point at which it decided what it was allowed to lose — and he gives the arithmetic out loud and says a town like this is the last place on the coast anybody can ask.** The holder says this yard does not know, and that it said the same in that market on the thirteenth of August and is not going to say it twice about the same thing, and that the sheet of the fifteenth of August carries the answer and the reason on its face in about nine words; **and then he gives out of the yard's own book a second-column line with no name in it — a line of places kept by more than one community for longer than any book in that office goes back cannot have been taken out of those communities' hands without somebody having had to decide to do it — and says the reason against it is not going into this street and that he is not going to call a fact an answer in a market.** At about half past eleven **Denzil Hare, of the third lay, about fifty, asks whether the case of five bells is coming up, and is told that it is at about five and a half fathoms inside four hundred yards off the outer marks and is on nobody's schedule, and that four people own it and wrote a line and signed it, and that the holder is not going to say what the line says in a market and has not asked to have it taken off and is not going to ask twice**, and that it was never on the holder's book and he is not going to be a man who puts it on one in December to look busy. **On the Thursday, on the rota day, the cistern is charged and walked on none, and the far bench is occupied and was not at it on the Wednesday at any hour, and at about half past four the holder says out loud, in about eleven people's hearing, that the chandler's note is not re-written, and why, and that this yard has now said it three times in a market and is not going to say it a fourth time.**

### Chapter 200 — A Name at the Head of a Form That Is an Office, and a Reason Against the Line
Friday the fourth and Saturday the fifth of December, on the face of the second seam, at the head of this dock, and in the shed. **Gross on the board fourteen, nine of it paid at the mole and the yard's being three, and nobody in the water, and the day went at the speed of a day with a man on the pump and a woman on the line.** At about half past two in the afternoon, while the tide was making, a half-sheet comes up the hill in the ordinary post with **the name of a court in a city at the head of it and the name of that court at the foot and no signature anywhere.** **It says the matter of the ASTER GANNET stands before that court and is not closed; that a person entered in it as an interested party is not examined for the full Second Bell license while it stands; that the holder of the writ is so entered by the making of a copy of a record, the entry being on the face of that copy, and the entry is dated the twenty-fourth day of November, which is the day the copy came off the counter; that he was put to the examination once before and did not satisfy it and that court knows nothing whatever of his trade; THAT IT IS NOT A SUSPENSION, NOT A PENALTY AND NOT A FINDING AGAINST ANYBODY, AND THAT A PERSON WHO IS NOT EXAMINED IS NOT A PERSON WHO HAS BEEN REFUSED; and that the writ is not before that court, is not a Second Bell license, does not lead to one and renews nothing, and that nothing in the paper touches the second season of that writ; and that a copy of the record is to be produced to that court on the fourth day of January, and a party which does not produce it is not fined.** The ship's hand says three things: that it is not the writ and that it says so twice; that it is not a penalty and not a refusal and that a man who is not examined is not a man who has been found wanting; and that the date on it is the date of the counter and the whole of the chain is a penny of postage, an application in their own hand, and a room asked for on a reeve's steps. **And the holder enters, in his own left hand and before any figure, that he was put to the examination in the September of two years back and did not satisfy it, and that the writ was granted eight months afterwards, and that this yard is not going to say in a street in January that the writ did this — and he reads the sixth paragraph twice before he does.** At about six, with the market gone in and about eleven people in the street, **the read-out, once, in about nine minutes, and not one line of the second column is read out in it.** At about half past nine in the shed, with the lamp lit and the day's entries read back by the ship's hand and after her by the holder and nobody else in the room, **the whole of the three weeks goes into the first column and four lines into the second, and then the holder stands up and puts the half-sheet of the court on the table squared to the edge of the pump, the way she puts a sheet down, and does not say anything else and does not sit down again.** The batch closes on four marks and six shillings and five pence in the bag, 717p, and on the standing fact that a working harbor of this coast is poorer on the fifth of December than it was on the twentieth of October, and that the case of five bells is on nobody's schedule, and that the two figures for the bar of gravel are both in a city and neither is withdrawn, and that the plate note stands open, and that a copy of a record is to be produced to a court in a city on the fourth day of January, and that this party has not asked any office for anything in aid of it. **NO SHEET WENT UP ON ANY WALL IN THIS HARBOR IN THREE WEEKS AND NONE CAME OFF, AND THAT IS ENTERED, AND THE VOLUME'S FINAL CHAPTER IMAGE IS NOT ON ANY PAGE OF IT.**

## Volume 04, Batch 0004 (Chapters 181–190) — complete. Movement 4, the faultreader in a room, which is the volume's midpoint.

**37,052 words across ten chapters**, Thursday the twenty-second to Tuesday the tenth of November 2026, measured with `wc -w` including headings, against the 2,200–3,200 guideline. **THE FIGURE WAS 36,882 BEFORE THE BATCH 0004 REVIEW'S REPAIRS AND IS 37,052 AFTER THEM.** **SIX CHAPTERS ARE INSIDE THE GUIDELINE AND FOUR ARE PAST IT, AND THE FOUR ARE PAST IT FOR A REASON THAT IS ON THE BATCH'S FACE AND IS NOT EXCUSED: 184 5,862 · 181 4,401 · 190 4,109 · 188 3,782 are past it; 183 3,672 · 182 3,429 · 185 3,236 · 186 3,006 · 189 2,983 · 187 2,572 are inside. 184 is the volume's midpoint and is one room over the tide-gang store across two days, and it carries the reading of the whole crew's book and the seam and the question and the sentence of the eighteenth of July read back in a room, and no part of it could be taken out without dropping a beat. 181 carries a clerk in a doorway, a paper served, a day of work and an arm. 188 carries a wall at eight in the morning and a day at a seam. 190 carries the read-out and the whole of three weeks. NO SCENE WAS CUT TO SHORTEN ANY CHAPTER AND NO COST WAS DROPPED TO BRING ONE INSIDE A NUMBER.** The hearing room is used on **two** days, the twenty-eighth and the twenty-ninth of October. The market wall is touched **once**, on the fifth of November, in the open air. The market office is used on **no day** in this batch. No two of the three rooms share a day, and it is verified a fifth time.

**THE CALENDAR, AND IT IS THE ONE THING IN THIS BATCH A READER CAN CHECK AGAINST THE VOLUME'S OWN LOCK AND FIND FAULT WITH.** Chapters 171 to 190 run on a grid three days out from the one `outline/volume-04.md` printed in lock L1, and the change enters with Chapter 171. **Lock L1 has been amended at its foot: the chapters decide a date, the change is recorded there with the reason it is not repaired in the text, and every chapter from 191 onward takes its days from Chapter 190.** The batch's own days are the days the chapters state and are set out in full at `state/continuity.md` § 1.

**THE SHAPE OF THE BATCH, SAID PLAINLY ONCE: THIS BATCH HAS AN ACTION CHAPTER AND THE PREVIOUS THREE BATCHES DID NOT. Chapter 181 carries a pump's handle coming round through the quarter-turn that is in it and striking a forearm across the bone; Chapter 188 carries a day of work with the jobs swapped and a hirer who said the day went slower than a day with three on it. There is no training chapter and no recovery chapter, and Chapter 189 is the nearest thing to a recovery chapter in three batches: a bonesetter's return that is not charged, and a wharf day refused.** `outline/volume-04.md` § *Costs this volume may not waive* is **PAID IN FULL IN MOVEMENT 4** — an injury to a named working crew member with a real cost and a real bill, one bad day, the cost of a hearing as a body's time, and the tide-sickness continuing in the second column with a reason against the line and no name in it — **and it is paid and it is not to be paid a second time, and Movement 5 may not use another injury as its own cost.**

**THE MONEY, AND IT CLOSES CHECKABLE ON THE PAGE IN PENCE: A MARK AND TWELVE SHILLINGS in a bag on the evening of Tuesday the twentieth of October, and A MARK AND EIGHT SHILLINGS AND SIXPENCE in it on the evening of Tuesday the tenth of November. A mark is a hundred and sixty pence and a shilling is twelve, so the whole of the arithmetic in this batch hangs on those two figures: 304p · 784p · 742p · 102p · 102p · 102p · 902p · 902p · 262p.** **THE BATCH CLOSES FORTY-TWO PENCE LIGHTER THAN IT OPENED, AND THE WHOLE OF THE DIFFERENCE IS THREE SHILLINGS AND SIXPENCE, AND THAT FIGURE IS A BONESETTER'S BILL PAID ON THE TWENTY-FOURTH OF OCTOBER, AGAINST FOUR PENCE OF POSTAGE AS THE WHOLE COST OF THE TWENTY DAYS BEFORE THAT. NO FIGURE IN THE BAG EXCEEDED SIX MARKS AND TWELVE SHILLINGS AT ANY POINT IN THE BATCH, AND THE HIGHEST IT STANDS IS FIVE MARKS AND EIGHT SHILLINGS AND SIXPENCE ON THE EVENING OF THE SIXTH OF NOVEMBER.** The chain: a wharf day on the twenty-third, gross ten, the yard's **three**, and the union lets go at half past two; **a bonesetter's bill of three shillings and sixpence on the twenty-fourth**; a wage of four on the twenty-seventh, column **twenty-four lines and ninety-six marks**, eighteen envelopes; **a wage of four on the third of November NOT PAID and no envelope made, which is the fourth week of the year with no envelope standing against it, and the column does not move**; a day at the second seam's own face on the sixth, gross seventeen, the yard's **five**, worked with the jobs swapped; a wage of four on the tenth, column **twenty-five lines and a hundred marks**, nineteen envelopes. **THE WHOLE OF THE MONEY THAT HAS COME INTO THIS YARD SINCE THE FIFTEENTH OF AUGUST IS THIRTY-ONE MARKS; THE WHOLE THAT WENT OUT IN THIS BATCH IS EIGHT MARKS AND FORTY-TWO PENCE, being two wages of four marks, a bonesetter's bill of three shillings and sixpence, and nothing at all else.**

**THE DUPLICATION AUDIT WAS RUN IN THREE PASSES, THE METHOD THE BATCH 0003 REVIEW LEFT BEHIND: 354 paragraphs of twelve words or more compared byte for byte across the ten chapters, ZERO byte-identical duplicates after one in-place repair; the same 354 keyed on its first forty words and then run pairwise for proper containment, ZERO opening matches and ZERO containment pairs inside the batch after that repair; and every exact sentence of eight words or more, 557 compared, 33 repeated at the final pass and 34 at the first, EVERY ONE OF THEM THIS YARD'S STANDING INVENTORY, THE BAG FIGURE AT THE HEAD OF A CHAPTER, OR A FORM OF THE HOUSE'S OWN SENTENCE ABOUT THE HARBOR'S POVERTY. ONE DUPLICATE PARAGRAPH WAS FOUND AND REPAIRED — an identical sentence about Alden Marr at the far bench in Chapters 186 and 190, the first instance left exactly as it is and the second varied in the yard's own idiom with no fact added and no fact removed — AND ONE SENTENCE REPEATED INSIDE A SINGLE CHAPTER WAS VARIED IN PLACE. The full result, the method and the reason the remaining thirty-three are not repaired are at `state/continuity.md` § 10.** The read-out device was used **once**, at the head of the dock on the evening of the tenth of November, and not one line of the second column was read out in it. **The reading of the crew's book in Chapter 184 is NOT a use of that device and the state files say so.** The read-back runs in **four** of the ten chapters, 181, 185, 188 and 190 — **the figure of one that stood here is withdrawn and was wrong**, and a plain string search for *read it back*, *read them back* and *read back after her* across the ten chapters returns `chapter-0181.md:77`, `chapter-0185.md:49`, `chapter-0188.md:63` and `chapter-0190.md:55` and nothing else. **The clerk's reading an entry back in a doorway or a room in Chapters 181 and 184 is his own trade's practice and is not this device.**

### Chapter 181 — A Day to Be Named, and a Paper Served in the Doorway

Thursday the twenty-second and Friday the twenty-third of October, at the mole's steps, in the doorway of the shed, and on a wharf on the boards. **The cistern behind the engine house was charged at no figure by strangers on the Thursday morning, the twentieth since the third of August, and this party walked none of the twenty. Alden Marr is at the far bench on the Thursday and is not asked for anything and asks nothing.** At about eleven on the Thursday **Ambrose Sell, clerk of the circuit office, comes up the hill and gives his name and the name of his office, and says four things: that a day is to be named and that he is not going to name it in a doorway because a day named in a doorway is a day a man can refuse before he has heard it; that a paper will be served naming it; that this yard's book is to be read whole and unshortened from the first of May to the last page and that this office has decided that and has not asked whether the party minds, and that he is saying it a day early so the party has a night to think about the parts it would have left out; and one warning, given once, that whatever goes into the record is what a clerk can enter and not what a clerk can be asked about afterwards.** Calder Marr answers in about two hundred words — that the second column is the only place this party has written down what it could not check, and that it is not going to leave a line out and is not going to soften one — and the clerk reads it back and enters it. And a party may be represented, and this party has said in September that it is not going to be, and he does not ask again. **On Friday at a quarter to eight in the morning the same clerk serves a paper in the doorway in about two people's hearing and does not read it out of it, and the party enters the day and the room — the twenty-eighth and the twenty-ninth of October, the hearing room over the tide-gang store — in the first column and enters why.** And then the day of work: a pump hired by a man of the third lay for a vessel with a started plank, **gross on the board ten, the yard's being three**, and at about half past two **a copper union on the discharge lets go under pressure and the pump's handle comes round through the quarter turn that is in it and strikes the left forearm of this yard's own shipwright across the bone about two inches above the wrist, and she says, before anybody has said anything, that it is the bone and not the joint, and asks that it be said on the boards in that order.** The man finishes the pumping himself with the pump on a bar and pays the day's figure at the figure without anybody arguing about the quarter of an hour that was left on the boards. **Four marks and twelve shillings is in the bag, and three shillings and sixpence goes out of it on the Saturday.**

### Chapter 182 — Three Shillings and Sixpence, and What a Clerk Can Enter

Saturday the twenty-fourth and Sunday the twenty-fifth of October, in a room at the back of a chandler's shop off the market, and in the shed. **At about ten on the Saturday afternoon a bonesetter comes up this hill on foot, looks at an arm in a shed for about eleven minutes, and charges three shillings and sixpence, which is paid out of the bag at ten past four at her own counter in her own hand.** **Hessa Barlow, forty-four, of this harbor, with a room at the back of a chandler's shop off the market, and a name on a painted board over that door since before either brother in the shed was born.** She says the joint is all right and everything one can do with a joint goes on, that the bone wants to be in one position for six weeks, that the forearm is not going to be a hand that takes a pump handle in a month, and that it is a quarter of the year gone out of the left side of a shipwright and there is nothing in the world she can do about any part of it; and she says the word *quarter* is to go in the book in the ship's own hand, because a quarter is a figure and a month is not. **And on the Sunday the two of them do the thing neither of them had thought to do in nine weeks, which is to go through the second column and find out how much of it can go into a record: a line, a date and an hour is what any clerk in any office on this coast can enter, and a reason is a paragraph and there is no column for it.** Five checkless lines, four with no name against them, one of the fourteenth of August of which the man who wrote it can bring up the first four words, and not one reason. **AND THE CLERK'S OWN WARNING OF THE THURSDAY AND THEIR OWN DISCOVERY OF THE SUNDAY ARE THE SAME FACT FOUND FROM TWO DIRECTIONS, AND THAT IS ENTERED.** The bonesetter says the return on the fourteenth day, Saturday the seventh of November, will not be charged and that she would be annoyed if anybody tried to thank her. **Four marks and eight shillings and sixpence is in the bag, and a wage of four marks falls on Tuesday the twenty-seventh and there is enough to pay it.**

### Chapter 183 — The Twenty-Fifth Wall, and a Wage That Goes In

Monday the twenty-sixth and Tuesday the twenty-seventh of October, at the mole's steps, at the head of the dock, and in the shed. **The wall behind the reeve's bench came down bare at eight, the twenty-fifth Mondays since the first of May, the clerk kept his own page and did not mention it, and the next sitting is on Wednesday morning at ten and is not in this party's keeping. The cistern was charged on the Monday, the twenty-first since the third of August, walked on none. Alden Marr is at the far bench.** And there is a man on the mole's steps who is not usually on it: **Fenn Ardle, forty-three, of the second lay, with a boat of about seventy tons and a boy of seventeen on the second hand**, who says two days, Wednesday and Thursday, and that the officer of that office came up on the Friday and told him before he told anybody else, and that he is out of a Tuesday on the water on the twenty-seventh and has told him that and it is written nowhere he can see, **and that a thing nobody has written down is a thing that did not happen in about four years.** Calder Marr says he is not going to ask that office for a tenth thing in that ledger and has not asked it for anything since the fourth of October, and is asking the man what his Tuesday is worth, and is not going to be thanked for it and is not going to say sorry, because a man who says sorry in a market for a Tuesday he did not take gets a Tuesday back for nothing. **And at a quarter to eleven Ors Kest says one thing in the open air, in about four people's hearing and about eleven people's near enough to hear it: that he put the man on those steps and told him on the Friday and not on the Monday morning because a man told on a Friday has a Saturday to be angry about; that the loss is not in his ledger because that ledger is spent; that a thing which cannot be entered cannot be produced and a thing which cannot be produced cannot be argued about; and that the repair account of that office has been empty since the middle of February and he would rather say that out loud to about eleven people than have it found out in a market in March.** The yard took nothing, was given nothing and asked for nothing. **And on the Tuesday the wage of four marks goes in at about eleven, the column is twenty-four lines and ninety-six marks, eighteen envelopes, three weeks fallen due, and eight shillings and sixpence is in the bag.** And at half past eight that night the two of them say out loud, once, what is coming on Wednesday and Thursday: the book goes down whole, and five bare lines are going into a record in a city with no reason against any of them, and the reason does not travel and the line does, and the third column is at the top of a page in a boy's hand and nobody fills it.

### Chapter 184 — The Whole of It, Whole and Unshortened, in a Room Over a Tide-Gang Store

Wednesday the twenty-eighth and Thursday the twenty-ninth of October, in the hearing room over the tide-gang store on the west basin, and on the boards. **THE VOLUME'S MIDPOINT. The wall behind the reeve's bench has been bare since Monday the twenty-sixth. About nine people are in the room on the first day and about eleven on the second, the tide-gang store is shut behind them, and no copy of any record is made in that room and none is given to any party and none is asked for.** The party is not represented. **Ambrose Sell writes the date and the hour before he says anything, says the book is to be read by the man who keeps it because a book read out by a stranger is a document, says he will enter a line, a date and an hour and NOT a reason, and gives the reason in his own trade's words: a record has a column and the column is the width of my page, and if I take four words out of a man's paragraph then in about four years a man in a city thinks the man wrote four words and he did not.** **AND THEN THE HOLDER READS HIS OWN BOOK OUT LOUD FOR THREE HOURS AND FIFTY MINUTES, WHOLE AND UNSHORTENED, FROM THE FIRST OF MAY TO THE LAST PAGE, IN THE ORDER IT IS IN, INCLUDING THE FOUR RETURNS WITH THE DAYS AGAINST THEM, THE COUNTING RULE, THE SHEET OF THE TWENTY-FIFTH OF AUGUST, THE DAY OF THE EIGHTEENTH OF AUGUST, THE ELEVEN WORDS OF THE TWENTY-NINTH OF SEPTEMBER, AND THE FIVE CHECKLESS LINES OF THE SECOND COLUMN AS BARE LINES.** The clerk asks twice for a thing to be read a second time and both times it is read a second time. **THE THIRD COLUMN AT THE HEAD OF A PAGE, FOURTEEN LINES IN THE RIGHT HAND OF A BOY OF TWENTY-TWO DATED FROM THE SECOND OF MAY, IS NOT READ BY THE HOLDER. He says read it. The clerk reads it in a flat voice, because it is a page and a clerk reads pages, and says in the room that this office cannot make that boy come and cannot make anybody ask him, and enters that the three nots of the eighth of June stand and that this office is not going to act on them.** And at four o'clock the clerk asks the holder what a second column is; the holder begins an answer and stops; the answer is entered as not given; **and the clerk then asks the ship's hand, and she answers it in one sentence, and it is entered that the answer was given by the ship's hand and not by the holder of the writ, and about eleven people look at the two of them, and the holder says nothing at all.** And on the Thursday the cistern is charged at no figure and this party is in a room and does not walk it, the twenty-second since the third of August. **AND THE SEAM.** The clerk asks whether the party says anything about the notice of the tenth of September, which is the paper the whole sitting is built out of, and Calder Marr says: there is a join in it, and gives four grounds — the colour of the ink in the first three paragraphs and the sixth against the fourth, the fold of the sheet at that paragraph the other way from the others, the opening and shutting of the paper at that fold, and the head of that paragraph starting a third of an inch lower than the head of the third, which is what a clerk does at a different sitting with a different nib, and he has watched about four hundred men do it — **and then says, in the same breath, that it does not say which of the two hands is right, or why, or whose the two hands were.** **AND THE CLERK SAYS THAT HE HAD THAT PAPER SINCE THE TENTH OF SEPTEMBER AND COULD SEE THE TWO INKS AND DID NOT NEED THE HOLDER OF THE WRIT TO COME UP A HILL AND POINT AT THEM, AND ENTERS THE FACT IN THAT ORDER, THE CLERK FIRST AND THE HOLDER SECOND, AND GIVES HIS REASON: that if it is left out of the record then in about four years the only thing in the bundle anybody is proud of is a thing a man of this harbor put there.** Calder Marr says write it. **Then the question — what does it mean — is asked, having been written in the clerk's own page first, and the answer is *I do not know*, three times, with the reason: that his trade finds out that two things were not made at one time and does not find out which of the two is the true one, and he has never once pretended to anybody that it did.** And then **the entry of the second column of the eighteenth of July is read out of his own book in a flat voice by a clerk who is not doing a part, and the line of the twenty-third of July with it, that the party did not stop him and has not shortened it and was wrong in three parts out of four and has not taken it back — and he does not deny it.** Nothing was decided. Nobody was convicted. **And on the boards at half past two on the Thursday afternoon the shipwright says the one thing she says about the woman: a form with her hand on it was in that room and she was not in that room, and it is the same as the eleven words of the sixteenth of October and it is not the same as the eleven words, and she wants the difference written down while the two of them can both still remember the room.**

### Chapter 185 — The Most Dangerous Column in This Harbor

Friday the thirtieth and Saturday the thirty-first of October, in the shed, and on the mole's steps. **Alden Marr is at the far bench on the Friday and is not asked for anything and asks nothing. Nothing happened on the Saturday and the party entered that, with the reason against the line, that a day on which nothing happens still happened.** And at about eight in the evening on the Friday the ship's hand puts her forearm on the table in a sling and says the thing she has been carrying since eleven o'clock on the Wednesday morning, and says first that she has been trying since Wednesday afternoon to find a way of saying it that does not sound like a frightened woman and cannot, and would rather say it badly than say it well and be listened to in the wrong tone. **THE SECOND COLUMN IS THE MOST DANGEROUS COLUMN IN THIS HARBOR, and it was not in September, and the yard wrote on the sixth of June that it is the only place a man in this harbor is allowed to say that he does not know and mean it, and on Wednesday a man said it in a room and a clerk wrote it down and it is in a record in a city and it cannot be got off.** And the half of it she did not see coming: the crew is two and a half and one of the two and a half has a bone cracked about two inches above her wrist and a figure of three shillings and sixpence and a quarter of a year out of the left side, and she is the tender and the pump and the fall and the board and the slate and the standing shutdown and there is nobody in this harbor the yard can hire to do any of it. **AND FOUR PEOPLE OF THIS COAST ARE ALIVE IN THIS TOWN WITH NO BOAT AND NO WAGES AND ABOUT NINE MARKS BETWEEN THEM, AND THEY WROTE A LIMIT THEMSELVES AND SIGNED IT, AND THE LIMIT IS NOT IN THE RECORD, BECAUSE A LIMIT IS A REASON AND REASONS DO NOT GO INTO A RECORD, AND IT CANNOT BE PUT THERE, BECAUSE THIS YARD HOLDS A WRIT AND A WRIT IS A CHANNEL AND A MAN WITH A WRIT IS EXACTLY THE THING THEY WROTE THE LINE AGAINST.** And she says there is a third thing and it is that he is going to keep saying it, that he is right to, and that in about four years a man in a city is going to read five bare lines and is not going to know whether they are the work of a man who was careful or the work of a man who had somebody to be careful about, and that the reason is not that anybody lied to him. **And the shape of the sentence is said about it: both of those are true and neither of them cancels the other, and there is no third thing to say and she has looked, and nobody improved on either half.** And she will not fill the third column and will point at the page of the seventeenth of October if somebody asks her in a room in four years. **And then she puts a slate and a piece of chalk on the table: three nights on the mole's steps, every pump that goes off and every boat that goes out and every boat that comes in, with the hour, and no reason against any of it — because that is the first thing anybody learns about a listening buoy and a buoy is a book of hours and a man who will not write the hours down, and the rest of it needs a crew that keeps them and there is no crew and she is not going to let him have the other thing.** He does the three nights: nine pumps, four boats out, three in, sixteen lines, no reason against any of them.

### Chapter 186 — A Man at the Head of a Dock Who Did Not Say the Yard Was Wrong

Sunday the first and Monday the second of November, at the head of the dock, at the mole's steps, and in the shed. **And on the Sunday, with the market gone in and about eleven people in the street, Gwil Lasker, thirty-six, of the first lay, with a boat of about a hundred and twenty tons and a man of forty on the second hand, who has crossed this bar twenty-one springs and has never once said out loud that the yard's figure was wrong, says that a yard which has been in a room and come out with a clerk saying that a clerk could see what the man could see is a yard whose figures are worth what the clerk's are — and says first, and says out loud, that he is not saying this yard was wrong, and that he is not the man of the third lay who has been waiting since the ninth of July for something to be wrong with.** His reason in full: a man in a city is going to read two lines on the same page and take the first off and leave the second on, and he does not know what he would do if he were that man with a book of his own, and he would do it in about four seconds and would not be lying when he did it. **And he says he has crossed this bar twenty-one springs and has never once said out loud that the yard's figure was wrong, and that at twenty-two he took a sounding off it off a master's own figure at that master's own asking and was a foot and a half out in a slack water and did not tell him, and that the said master had it himself inside a month off a man out of a city, and that neither of them has said one word about it to the other since — and he gives his name before he is asked for it.** **THE ERROR HE CONFESSES IS HIS OWN. Until the Batch 0004 review this entry gave him the holder's own error — the word *chain* and three parts out of four in one sentence — which is Calder Marr's, at `chapters/volume-03/chapter-0138.md:27`, and is the line the clerk reads back in the room at `chapter-0184.md:137`; two men carrying the same mistake is the one thing the volume's reversal turns on, and `chapter-0186.md:17` and its first-column entry are corrected.** Calder Marr does not thank him, does not let eleven people hear him say that a man who has crossed this bar twenty-one times has come about on the figures, **and says the other half: the figure is not withdrawn and is not going to be, the pilot's figure is not withdrawn, neither can check the other and neither has ever pretended to — and both of those are true and neither of them cancels the other. He answers in about two hundred words, and the first column now says so.** Nobody in the street said anything, which is what about eleven people say about two men who have both told the truth about the same thing. **The wall behind the reeve's bench came down bare on the Monday, the twenty-sixth Mondays since the first of May, and the last thing that was ever on that wall was a sitting two days ago that is now a bundle in a city. The cistern was charged, the twenty-third since the third of August, walked on none. Alden Marr is at the far bench.** And the whole of the two days goes into the first column against itself, with the reason: **that a yard which has spent four volumes saying that its own record is the only kind anybody can check has come out of a room less able to be checked, and that it happened by the party's own method, in public, in about nine and about eleven people's hearing, and that nobody was convicted of anything and nothing was decided and no office was a villain in it, and that a man who is the best reader of joins in this harbor was made less credible by a thing he is good at and said so himself in a room and cannot unsay it, and that the yard is not going to be told it is sorry about it in a market and is not going to thank anybody for it and is not asking.**

### Chapter 187 — An Envelope Not Made, and a Column Nobody Is Going to Fill

Tuesday the third and Wednesday the fourth of November, in the shed, and at the head of the dock. **THE WAGE OF FOUR MARKS DID NOT GO IN ON TUESDAY THE THIRD AND NO ENVELOPE WAS MADE, and the number of weeks fallen due this year with no envelope standing against them is now FOUR — the fifteenth of August, the fifth of September, the sixth of October and the third of November — entered in the same breath and not one after the other.** The column stands at twenty-four lines and ninety-six marks, the fifth of March the oldest, eighteen envelopes on a bench, and **eight shillings and sixpence in the bag, which is one hundred and two pence, and a wage of four marks is six hundred and forty pence and the yard is **five hundred and thirty-eight** pence short of it (**the figure of six hundred and thirty-eight that stood here and in two chapters was arithmetically wrong and is corrected; the full account is at `state/continuity.md` § *Batch 0004 review repairs*, item 2**), and this party entered on the sixth of October that it would enter the day of a working Tuesday unpaid in the first column with the figure and the reason rather than wait for a day it does not know is coming, and has entered it again because it came.** And at half past seven on the Wednesday evening the two of them have the argument the third column has been waiting for since the seventeenth of October. **He says then somebody has to be appointed. She says no, twice, and the second no is not the reason of the seventeenth of October.** Hers: that the column is a page where a man wrote down what he said when he was not in the boat, that it has been worth what it is worth because nobody ever filled it but him, that on Wednesday a clerk read fourteen lines of it out loud in a room in a flat voice and entered that the office cannot make the boy come and cannot make anybody ask him, **and that there is no more of that column in this harbor that anybody can add to, and whatever goes in it now goes in it after a room has been in it.** And when he suggests that she put her nine sentences in it, **she says that is the worst thing anybody has said in that shed since the first of May, and that she has already said out loud in this yard in her own hand what the nine sentences are and why they are not going in a page, and that if the column is going to be filled by somebody then somebody is going to be reading it and is going to be deciding what goes in and what does not, and a page one person decides is a page one person owns, and that is a man with a book and not a column.** And then nobody is appointed, and the reason: **a column nobody fills is a column somebody else fills, and the somebody else is a clerk with a pen, and a clerk with a pen is not a bad thing and is not a villain and is the only way a record gets made at all, and the day he fills it is the day this yard stops being able to say that the one honest column in it is his.** The first column says plainly that the party does not know where the boy is and has not asked and is not going to send for him, and the three nots of the eighth of June stand.

### Chapter 188 — A Name for a Reason, and Five Marks at the Second Seam

Thursday the fifth and Friday the sixth of November, at the wall outside the market office, on the mole's steps, and on the face of the second seam. **The cistern was charged on the Thursday, the twenty-fourth since the third of August, walked on none.** And at about eight in the morning, before the market was off the boards, **a man of the first lay, about fifty-five, with a coasting boat of his own and a letter from a city in his coat, is standing in front of the unsigned finding on that wall reading the reason on the page out loud, and asks this yard for the name of the woman who wrote it.** He says he has been at four doors in three days and this is the fifth and it is a wall, and that a thing on a wall with no name on it is a thing he cannot be made to answer for, and that he has a letter that a man in a city is going to act on the strength of that sheet. **Calder Marr says no in about four minutes and gives the whole of it: she is not on this crew and not of this yard and not a holder and not on the four names, and the reporting clause does not reach her and never did; she left this crew in her own words on the twenty-third of July and that is a different thing from anything that has happened to her since and he is not going to put the two together in a street; she is owed forty marks first after food since the fifteenth of October and not one mark has been paid and the six marks and two days have not been offered and have not been reduced and he is not going to offer them in a street in November because it is the same price and it has not moved; the sheet is unsigned because she asked for it to be unsigned and he is not going to have it signed or taken down; and this party was refused by her in a room open to anybody on the twenty-ninth of September and has asked her nothing since, because a party which has been refused once and then tells a man in a street where the woman is has decided that a person is a door.** And the man's answer is the cost of the whole month: **that in July the same answer sounded like a yard being careful and that on the fifth of November it sounds like a wall, and that he knows a page in a city is a sheet he can be made to answer for and a wall is not, and that he would rather have the page, and would rather have it from this yard than from anybody else, and is not going to pretend he would rather have a yard than a wall because he has been in four rooms in this harbor this month.** And Calder Marr says he has got it the right way round and has not got a better version of it, and adds the one thing that is against this yard and not for it: **a sheet with no name on it is a sheet a man cannot be made to answer for, a page in a city is a sheet a man can be made to answer for, this yard has spent four volumes saying the second one is the only kind of record anybody can check, and it went into a room on the twenty-ninth of October and came out with that, and he is the man who put it there, and he would rather have the wall than the page and has not got a reason for that which is worth anything.** And on the Friday **a day at the second seam's own face, gross seventeen, the yard's being five, with the jobs swapped: the holder runs the pump, which he has not done since the second of May and did badly for the first hour, and the ship's hand keeps the book and the count and the times and hauls the line over with her right hand; and the hirer knew about the arm and did not ask about it and said the day went slower than a day with three on it, and the holder said that it did, and the man said he knew it did and had had a yard of two before.** The first column re-enters as a carried fact that the yard now holds two documents and not one, and that a form is a form whichever office writes it.

### Chapter 189 — The Fourteenth Day, and a Wharf Day Refused

Saturday the seventh and Sunday the eighth of November, in a room at the back of a chandler's shop off the market, and in the shed. **And at about ten on the Saturday morning a bonesetter comes up this hill and looks at an arm in a shed for about nine minutes, on the fourteenth day, and does not charge for it, having said on the twenty-fourth that she would not, and says so again before she has looked at it.** She will not touch it: there is a thing in a bone that has to be left alone for fourteen days, and if anybody puts a thumb on it today that is the day it does not knit. **The strapping comes off on the twenty-first of November and not before**, and the figure of the three shillings and sixpence is paid and is not going to be charged again by her this winter, **and the figure of the rest of it is a quarter of a year out of the left side of the only shipwright on this coast who can hold a pump on a wharf for six hours, and that is not her figure and she does not want it in her book.** And she says a day of work this yard has refused is the most expensive thing anybody can hand you and nobody hands it to you for nothing. And Nessa Pike says the three shillings and sixpence and the quarter of a year go on the same line in the first column and not in two places, in her own hand, **and that a bone is not a bill and a bill is not a bone and a man who has the two in one line will end up arguing about the wrong one** — and adds that on Wednesday a man read her hand out of a book in a room for about an hour and a half and she sat in that room the whole time and could not check one figure of it. **And at about half past four on the Sunday afternoon a man of the first lay comes up this hill and offers a day, and it is about three marks on a wharf, and this yard refuses it and says the reason in nine words: a pump on a wharf takes two and one of them is her, and her forearm is strapped, and a hand that will not take a handle until the twenty-first of November at the earliest, and this party is not going to book a day it cannot work.** And the other half of the reason, which is in the same breath: that on the ninth of September a man of the first lay of about ninety tons asked this yard to look at a thing on his stem and this party said no and he said he had not expected it in October and would come again in the spring, and this party is entered that it is not going to be the yard that says yes to everything from November and then cannot do the February ones. He says that is a good answer and not a cheerful one. **And the second column carries the body's time: a man stood in a room for two days in the last week of October and read out of his own book for three hours and fifty minutes, and on the Wednesday night he did not sleep and on the Thursday night he did, and the party entered both halves of that on one line and is not going to name what it is in a shed, and that the four lines above the named line of the twentieth of July are still four and are still nameless and are not struck out.**

### Chapter 190 — The Twenty-Seventh Wall, and a Reason Against the Line

Monday the ninth and Tuesday the tenth of November, at the mole's steps, at the head of the dock, and in the shed. **The wall came down bare, the twenty-seventh Mondays since the first of May, and the last thing that was ever on that wall was a sitting four days ago that is now a bundle in a city. The cistern was charged at no figure by strangers and this party did not walk it, and no figure is entered against that charge. Alden Marr is at the far bench, the sixth and last of the three weeks.** And on the Tuesday **the wage of four marks goes in at about eleven, the column is twenty-five lines and a hundred marks, nineteen envelopes, four weeks fallen due, and a mark and eight shillings and sixpence is in the bag, which is two hundred and sixty-two pence, and the same bag held three hundred and four pence on the twentieth of October, and the whole of the difference is three shillings and sixpence and it is a bonesetter's bill on the twenty-fourth and it is the only money this yard has spent on itself in twenty-one days.** **And at about six, with the market gone in and about eleven people in the street, the two of them read the three weeks out at the head of this dock in about nine minutes — and that is what a reading at the head of a dock is, and nobody was asked to be quiet.** The shipwright says the figures: the bag, the column, four weeks fallen due, nineteen envelopes, three shillings and sixpence and a quarter of a year, five marks off the face of the second seam, three marks refused on the wharf on the twelfth of October and three marks refused again this week, nothing on the twenty-eighth and nothing on the twenty-ninth, the cistern charged twenty-four times since the third of August and walked on none, the wall twenty-seventh time, **the far bench occupied on the twenty-second and the twenty-sixth and the thirtieth of October and the second, the sixth and the ninth of November, and on no other day of the three weeks, and neither brother went over to the other on any of the six; a man of the second lay out of a Tuesday that is in no ledger and is in this yard's book; the thing none of them can check, which is a record in a city with five bare lines of the second column in it and a clerk's entry that he could see a join before the man who keeps the book pointed at it, and a market of the eighteenth of July read out in a room by a stranger; and a man of the first lay of about fifty-five at the wall outside the market office on the Thursday morning who said he would rather have the page than the wall, and was right, and whom neither of them is going to unsay.** **THE READ-OUT NAMED THE WRONG MAN UNTIL THE BATCH 0004 REVIEW: it gave the *page rather than the wall* sentence to Gwil Lasker, thirty-six, at the head of the dock a week ago, and it is the man of fifty-five at the wall, and `chapter-0190.md` is corrected in the read-out, in the holder's speech and in the first-column entry. THE READ-OUT ALSO GAVE THE CISTERN TWENTY-FOUR CHARGES AND THEN SAID IT WAS CHARGED AGAIN THAT MORNING, WHICH A READER COUNTS AS TWENTY-FIVE; IT NOW KEEPS THE TWENTY-FOUR AND SAYS THE PARTY IS NOT GOING TO STAND IN A STREET AND SAY WHETHER THAT MAKES IT TWENTY-FIVE, BECAUSE IT DID NOT SEE THE DAYS THOSE MEN COME ON. The full account of both is at `state/continuity.md` § *Batch 0004 review repairs*, items 3, 4 and 5.** And she says she is not going to read one line of the second column out and is not going to be asked to in a market and will point at a page in their own book if she is asked in a room in a city in four years. **And at about half past nine in the shed, with the lamp lit and the read-back done from the top by the ship's hand and after her by the holder and nobody else in the room, the whole of three weeks goes into the first column and four lines of it into the second, and the ship's hand stops the read-back at the Thursday of the twenty-ninth of October and says: a man of this yard was right about a thing in a room on the twenty-ninth of October, and the same man has been less believed in a market ever since, and both halves of that go on one line in the first column with a reason against it, and the reason is that a man in this harbor can get off a mole tomorrow and walk to that room and ask a clerk whether it is in his page, and can ask any man on that quay on the fifth of November whether a man of about fifty-five said at that wall what he said he was going to say, and that the cost of what happened in that room is public and is not a thing either of them can hold in a shed.** And the forward pull: **the question standing on the table is a document this party has never seen and has never asked for, a copy of a record about an object with the limits the record itself carries printed on the face of the copy, which would show that a manifest was altered and would not show who altered it, and which a man of the first lay of this harbor cannot check, and the party is not applying for one this month and has entered why and is not saying no to it in four years and is not saying yes either, and the release of that copy is not in this yard's keeping and has never been in it.** A working harbor is poorer at the end of these three weeks than it was at the start of them, and nothing this yard has done in three weeks has made it better, and nothing this yard published on a market wall in October has made it better.
