### Chapter 201 — The First Morning, and a Counter at Thirty-One
Sunday the sixth and Monday the seventh of December, in the shed, and at the mole's steps. **No day of work booked on either day, and the reason against the line is that a day on which nothing happens still happened, and that this is the first day of a month in which this yard is going to have to be able to be checked on any day of a week by anybody who can get off a mole.** Three sheets are on the bench where the pump used to be and none has been put away: the copy of a record of the twenty-fourth of November in six heads and about a page and a half, dated the date of a counter and not the date of a record; the acknowledgment of the twenty-ninth; and the half sheet of a court in a city of the fourth of December with the **fourth of January** on it. **Twenty-nine days, of which four are Tuesdays.** The holder enters, in his own left hand, with the date and the hour on it, that this party will do nothing in aid of the fourth of January, and gives the whole reason: **a thing done in order to be ready is a thing that arrives with a date on it**, and in about four years a clerk in a city would have a piece of paper with a date on it and this party's name at the head of it, and it would be in a file whatever happened to the yard. **The writ runs to midnight on the first of March and the two dates are not being joined.** The ship's hand puts the standing question once: it is standing, it is not going away because nobody asked it twice, and **if somebody asks it in a market in January the answer is the answer already on the page and it is not going to be a better one.** On the Monday the wall comes down bare for the thirty-first Monday since the first of May, the clerk keeps his own page and does not mention it, the cistern is charged on the rota day at no figure and walked on none, and the far bench is occupied from ten until about four and neither brother goes over. The read-back runs. **No money in and nothing out on either day; 717p in the bag, seventy-seven pence short of the wage, six weeks fallen due with no envelope standing against them.**

### Chapter 202 — A Form With No Date on It, and a Man Who Is Not the Man Who Directs It
Tuesday the eighth and Wednesday the ninth of December, in the shed, and on the mole. **The wage of four marks does not go in, no envelope is made, and the number of weeks fallen due with no envelope standing against them is SEVEN, all seven given in the same breath, and the bag was seventy-seven pence short of the wage.** At about eleven on the Wednesday morning a man comes up the mole on foot in a coat that has been in weather, with nothing in his hands until he is in the doorway, **and he does not give his name.** He gives the name of the office at the foot of the sheet and the reason he is not giving his own: a man's name at the foot of a thing is a thing a man in a city can hold for four years, which is the reason a clerk of that circuit office gave at this bench on the twenty-third of November and which the yard has in its first column in two hands. **The sheet is a sheet and a half with no signature anywhere and no date at the head, in the body or at the foot, and at the foot there is the name of an office; the name of that office is not on any page of this yard's book and is not printed here, and the reason is on the page and is not a defect. It is the form of an office in a city of the same kind as the form that acknowledged the discrepancy on the twenty-ninth of November and signed nothing, and it is NOT the circuit office, NOT the Court of Wreck Claims, NOT the harbor authority and NOT the coast office.** It proposes that this yard hold a copy of a record for the coast and be named in an entry as the person who holds it, and says the party is not to be examined, licensed, enrolled, paid or asked to find anything and is asked one question; **it says nothing about what follows an answer of no, and the date of the answer is the only date on it.** **He says the Crown's position in one sentence and one sentence only: testimony is safe in one place and nowhere else, and this harbor is not that one, and that is not an opinion, it is what three hundred years of an office is. Nobody in that shed said it was a lie and nobody said the man was anything at all.** He said he is not the man who directs it, and that he did not know what picking up a sheet off a bench is and the office does not say, and left the sheet on the bench and went down the mole and nobody followed him. The ship's hand reads it out once more and squares it and says: **the whole of the sheet is for a name; a room is a building and an address is a name; and a party which holds a copy of a record becomes the address of that copy, and that is true, and she tried all Wednesday to make it not true and could not.**

### Chapter 203 — The Harbor Authority Agrees, and Says What It Cannot Do, and Names the Price of the Rest
Thursday the tenth and Friday the eleventh of December, on the mole's steps, and in the shed. **The cistern is charged on the rota day and walked on none.** At about eleven, in the open at the foot of the mole's steps in about eleven people's hearing, the holder asks the officer of the harbor authority one question and says first what he is **not** asking: not an instrument, not to seat anybody, not to hold anything, and he gives the reason, including that the third lay of this harbor lost four days in June for the want of a room and that it will not happen again. **Kest says his office's view is that a thing kept in Morrow Quay is a thing kept in Morrow Quay**, and gives the reason: no office on this coast can get at a copy of a record kept in this place; a record in a city can be asked for and got at by a man with a carriage and a record in Morrow Quay can be got at by about four people, two of whom are the men on that mole; **and a man in a city who wants a thing in a fortnight will not send a carriage and will write to this harbor authority first, because that is the name a man writes first when he does not know any other, and that office will answer him and cannot tell him a thing about a record, and that is how a man in a city finds out where a thing is.** Then he names **what his office cannot do**: it cannot certify a copy of a record, cannot hold a record for the coast, **cannot enter this in his ledger because the ledger is spent and the ninth acknowledgement is spent and a tenth may not be entered and will not be**, cannot renew the writ of the first of May and is the only office that can, cannot overrule a seizure, cannot certify a glass witness — and he is not going to do the rest of it and pretend afterwards that it was part of it. **The price is named before he says yes, and it is not a morning of the tide gang and not a day of the third lay and not anybody's body: it is that his office becomes a name in this business, and every party who comes up that hill about anything at all is going to be told at the foot of those steps that this office has an opinion about a copy of a record, for years, including the ones who have nothing to do with this party.** He puts it on a page of his own and not in the ledger, **because a page of his own is not a record and cannot be produced on demand by anybody**, and says again that he is not a friend of this party and that the yard agrees with him. On the Friday the far bench is occupied from ten until about four, not asked, asking nothing, and **he was not at that bench on the Thursday at any hour**, and at about four a man comes up the hill about a day at the second seam on the Saturday at about three marks and the yard takes it. **THE FAR-BENCH LINE OF THE ELEVENTH IS NOW IN THAT CHAPTER'S FIRST COLUMN AND WAS NOT AT FIRST, AND IT IS ONE OF THE FOUR DAYS OF THE BENCH IN THIS BATCH; THE REPAIR AND ITS REASON ARE IN `state/continuity.md`.**

### Chapter 204 — Three Marks on a Board, and the Thing After the Gland Is the Hose
Saturday the twelfth and Sunday the thirteenth of December, on the face of the second seam, and in the shed. **The board for the day is twelve gross, and what comes off the top of a day rate comes off the top of it in the order it has come off since the third of May: the eighth, one. The share, one. The victuals of the day, one. A hired hand, three. A second hired hand, three. Nine of twelve paid at the mole and the yard's being three, which is four hundred and eighty pence, and the bag held seven hundred and seventeen on the Friday morning and the sum of the two is one thousand one hundred and ninety-seven, which is seven marks, six shillings and five pence.** A hand lead's line went over the side and back four times, nobody in the water, the count and the times onto a slate in the ship's hand and the slate into the book in her own writing and the holder reading it back after her. The hirer was of the second lay with a boat of about seventy tons and brought the pump and a man and **said he had asked for a yard**, and she tells him the day is going slower than a day with three on it and that the reason is in the book in the holder's hand and not in hers and that he would only have an opinion; he says he has not got one. **The day is booked by this party with a person in it and not a person short, and the cost of that is a day that went slower, and a day that goes slower is a day not earned whole and is entered as a day not earned whole and not as a day at a figure.** On the Sunday no day is booked, and the second column carries the gland and the hose: **five marks of compressor's gland standing on that page since the nineteenth of May, unpaid and useless, and the thing after the gland is the hose and this harbor has no hose**, and the yard is not going to price it and not going to buy one out of a day at a seam, and she said the rest of that sentence once and is not going to say it a second time in a December. **The form is still on the bench under the pump and has not been answered and has not been put in the stove.**

### Chapter 205 — Eleven Lines Beside a Form, and a Reason That Cannot Be Written Down
Monday the fourteenth and Tuesday the fifteenth of December, in the shed. **The wall comes down bare for the thirty-second Monday and the cistern is charged on the rota day and walked on none.** In the shed the ship's hand takes down the sheet of the twenty-second of November, folded once, and reads the eleven aloud: the place of the case of five bells; the four people it belongs to; **the limit they wrote themselves**; the plate at the fifth seam; the two objects on the crown of this mole; the boy; the forty marks standing whole to him; the forty marks first after food to a person of no office; the delay; the letter that was coming up this hill on the Saturday; and the two figures for the bar of gravel and the Kettle, which was not looked at. **Item ten has happened and came back no.** She reads the limit out once and it is that **the account is not to be read out, is not to go into a book that leaves this harbor, is not to be said to a man with a seal, and not one name to any office in any city**, and the holder says the fourth paragraph back as a sentence: a party which agrees to hold a copy of a record is a party that has put its name in a record. **THE DISAGREEMENT IS THE BATCH'S DISCOVERY: the yard cannot put the limit in the answer because the answer carries the name of the party, the place and the date, and a reason does not go into a record; and the yard cannot put its name to an answer without the limit in it, because that is what an answer is. And a place a copy of a record is kept in IS a room, and the eleven lines are a list of what does not go into a room, and every one of them is a thing this yard would be right to keep out of a room — so the eleven lines are the answer to that form, and the answer to that form is eleven lines this yard cannot put in an answer. NOBODY'S NAME WENT NEAR A PIECE OF PAPER ABOUT A FORM.** She is not going to write a no sentence this month, because a bare sentence in a form with a name at the foot of it will be read in about four years as a man who had somebody to be careful about. On the Tuesday the wage is not paid and the number of weeks fallen due with no envelope is EIGHT, all eight in the same breath, and the bag is five hundred and fifty-seven pence short. The read-back runs. **Item eleven has not been moved off that list in three weeks and is not going to be moved in a December.**

### Chapter 206 — A Sentence in a Street, and a Reason That Does Not Travel
Wednesday the sixteenth and Thursday the seventeenth of December, at the head of this dock, and in the shed. **No day of work booked on either day, and the reason against the line is that a day on which two things happened and neither of them was work has to be entered twice and the second time it says so.** At about half past eleven on the Wednesday, at the head of this dock in about eleven people's hearing, a man of the third lay asks what the yard is going to do about a thing on a bench up the hill and says he is not asking it for anybody and that he has had it from three people since Friday and has stopped repeating it back. **The holder answers in the street on purpose, and gives the reason: a thing said in a street is checkable by anybody who was in it, and a thing said in a shed is checkable by one other person.** He gives the whole shape of the sheet and says the answer will be in his own hand, is not an office's, is not signed by a clerk, and has no day on the sheet to be late — **and then says he is not going to be able to say today whether it is going to be a yes or a no, and that he is not going to be in a position to say in three weeks either, and that he would rather the man had it from him in a street than stand there working it out of his face for a month.** On the Thursday **two men of the first lay are saying that this yard has not said no, and one of them says the word *hold* twice, and this party does not go up and correct either of them.** The cistern is charged on the rota day and walked on none, and **the far bench was not occupied on the Wednesday or the Thursday at any hour, and the line is entered with no figure of what came before it: there are days of these three weeks the yard has not entered and is not going up that hill again to enter, and a man who comes up and sees that bench empty and asks what it was the day before is told that they do not know. THE FIRST RECORD SAID THAT THE LAST TIME BEFORE THOSE TWO DAYS THAT THE BENCH WAS EMPTY FOR TWO DAYS TOGETHER WAS IN THE MONTH OF SEPTEMBER PAST, AND THAT CLAIM IS WITHDRAWN IN THE CHAPTER AND HERE: THE FIFTEENTH IS UNSTATED IN THE BATCH, AS ARE EIGHT OTHER DAYS, AND NO CHAPTER SUPPORTS THE FIGURE.** The ship's hand says the read-back is deliberately absent, because a thing said in a street is read back by whoever was standing in the street and there were about eleven of them and not one of them is in that shed. **She gives the whole of the reason it cannot be put right: to say no in that street he would have to say the whole of it, including four people of this coast and a limit that does not go into a street, and the second he says that, three men in this town know a thing about four people who did not know it before Friday, and in about four years it will be a thing a man in a city can look up. HE CANNOT SAY NO IN THE STREET AND HE CANNOT SAY YES IN THE STREET AND HE CANNOT SAY HE HAS NOT DECIDED TWICE. The two columns are the reason against the line and the fact that a reason does not travel, and the gap between them is not closed and says so on the page.**

### Chapter 207 — A Counter off the Fish Market, and Two Prices That Are Not the Same Price
Friday the eighteenth and Saturday the nineteenth of December, at a counter in a room off the fish market, and in the shed. **The far bench is occupied on the Friday from ten until about four, not asked, asking nothing, and he was not at that bench on the Thursday at any hour.** At about ten the yard goes down to the counter, which is open to anybody from eight until the market is off the boards, **puts the copy of the record flat on it and reads none of it out, and nobody in that room hears one line of it.** A copyist of fifty-eight sits down on a stool she has not sat on for the last three people of that week and says **nobody has ever said to me at that counter that they did not want a copy.** Two questions, two answers in her own trade's words: **a copy is a copy, and nine copies are nine of the same words and not one of the nine checks anything, and what a man in nine places can check is nine people who each went and got it, and that is not a copying trade and is a fetching trade, and there is not one trade on this coast that does it, and the four people who could do it are alive in this town with no boat and about nine marks between them**; the paper is nine pence and forty of those is two shillings and a threepence, and **the price is nine men's time, and a man's time in a working port in December is a day, and a day at a good trade is about three marks on a board with the eighth and the share off the top, so the price of a page that anybody in nine places could check is the whole of a day's work at a seam.** And the other: **a page that nobody can check is a sheet and a shed and no money at all, and it is the one that cannot be shown to anybody, and a thing that cannot be shown to anybody is a thing that has not been made, and the two of those are the same sentence.** She says she could not tell him in a room which is better because it is not her trade to say. Then the third question, which is not about paper: **what does it cost to write a reason against a line and then to have the reason stop at the edge of the page** — and the answer is that a paragraph is a paragraph, she will write one for a penny like anything else, the good ones are worth about a penny more than the bad ones and that is what a trade is, **and the moment a reason goes on a page it is a paragraph, and a paragraph is a trade, and a trade travels, and a reason can be kept in a shed for nine years and cannot be kept in a paragraph.** And then, about sheets in general and not about this yard's: **a sheet with an office's name at the foot of it and no man's name on it anywhere is the easiest thing in the world for a copyist to sell, because there is nobody on it for her to ask where it came from, and those go out of a shop and into somebody's buyer's book and nobody ever knows.** The yard buys one sheet of her paper at a penny, into her own hand, for its own use and not for a copy of anything, **because this party cannot put a price in a record out of a person's mouth.** 1,196p in the bag.

### Chapter 208 — Two Marks Paid on Saturday and a Man Who Did Not Come on Monday
Sunday the twentieth and Monday the twenty-first of December, at the boards, in the shed, and on the mole. **No day of work booked on the Sunday, and the reason against the line is that a day on which nothing happens still happened, and that the Sunday was a day on which this yard was told a price it had not asked for and entered it before anybody asked it for anything.** At about nine at the boards a man of the second lay tells the ship's hand a thing and tells her and not the holder, and says he is telling her because she writes things down and not because he wants anything out of it. **He lightered alongside that mole on the sixth of November, he hired a man on the Saturday evening for the Monday, and he paid him then, two marks and his victuals, in his own hand out of his own pocket, and the man did not come at eight.** He says he is not going to say it is the hired man's fault and that he has been stood up by better men. **The hired man's reason, in his own words: if everything a yard says in this town goes into a book in a city then a day's work of mine goes into one too, and I have got a girl at a school in a town four days up this coast and I am not having her name read out in a town I have never been to. And I said that is a great deal of fuss about a day's work. And he said it is my day's work, and that is the end of it.** The man will not give the name and neither will the lighterman, and he says that if this party went down to that quay he would be the man who told it. **The price is entered on the Sunday night, in the holder's own hand, at her asking, before anybody asks for anything, and the yard is not going to pay it: a yard which pays a man's day for a thing it said in a street has bought the street, and the yard does not want it and cannot afford it, and the first of those three is the one that is on the page.** **THE CHAPTER NOW ALSO ENTERS, IN ITS PROSE AND IN ITS FIRST COLUMN, THAT THE SATURDAY EVENING OF THE NINETEENTH IS THE SECOND OF TWO DAYS ON WHICH THE SAME TWO MEN OF THE FIRST LAY WERE SAYING A THING ABOUT THIS YARD, THE FIRST BEING THURSDAY THE SEVENTEENTH WHEN THEY WERE SAYING THAT THIS YARD HAD NOT SAID NO AND ONE OF THEM SAID THE WORD *HOLD* TWICE; THAT THE YARD WENT UP ON NEITHER DAY; THAT THE REASON AGAINST BOTH LINES IS THAT A CORRECTION IS THE REASON AND THE REASON DOES NOT GO INTO A STREET; THAT THE FIRST OF THE TWO DAYS IS OUT OF THE YARD'S OWN EYES AND THE SECOND IS OUT OF WHAT THE LIGHTERMAN SAID AT THE BOARDS; AND THAT THE YARD HAS NOT GOT EITHER MAN'S NAME AND IS NOT GOING UP THE HILL FOR IT.** On the Monday the wall comes down bare for the thirty-third Monday, the cistern is charged and walked on none, **the far bench is occupied from ten until about four, not asked, asking nothing, and he was not at it on the Sunday at any hour**, and the two of them go down the hill past the west quay at about eight and the lighter is lying there with one man on it and neither brother says anything and neither stops. The read-back runs.

### Chapter 209 — A Letter With No Threat in It, and the One Place a Man in a City Can Look
Tuesday the twenty-second and Wednesday the twenty-third of December, in the shed, and on the mole. **The wage is not paid, no envelope is made, and the number of weeks fallen due with no envelope standing against them is NINE, all nine in the same breath, and the bag is five hundred and fifty-six pence short.** On the Tuesday the two of them enter that the board has nothing in it for five days after it, **and that a board with nothing in it for five days is a figure anybody can add up, and that a figure anybody can add up is the first one this year that this yard has not been able to stop happening.** At about ten on the Wednesday one sheet comes up the hill in the ordinary post, dated the twentieth: **a printer's letter.** It is not a threat and not a favor and the printer says so himself. **A man who sells ice bought a sheet at a counter off this fish market on the sixth of December, paid a penny, put it in a box of fish and sent it up the coast; it is a half sheet with the name of an office at the foot of it and no man's name anywhere on it; the printer keeps a list of everything that comes into his shop that anybody means to keep; and on the ninth of December a man came to that counter and bought type and nothing else and asked whether he could say where a copy of a record was, and wrote down the name of the town it came from and the day, and said thank you and bought his type.** His one question: **where is the copy of the record.** He is not asking for the sheet, is not asking this party to be examined or licensed or enrolled or paid, is not asking on anybody's behalf, and **is not going to write again until this party writes to him.** The sheet goes onto the bench beside the form with no date on it, and the acknowledgment of the twenty-ninth comes off the shelf, and the three lie in a line a foot apart for about four minutes and nobody says anything. **THE TURN OF THE BATCH: this yard did not write that acknowledgment, did not pay for it, did not ask for it and did not allow it, and it is the one page in this country by which a man in a city can find out where this yard is, and it did the whole of the work in nine days, and no office on this coast could have stopped it and no clerk and no form and no clause.** And: **a reply is a document with this yard's name at the foot of it** — if the holder writes to that printer then this shed has an address it made itself, on this yard's own paper, in its own hand, dated, and the office whose name is at the foot of the form on that bench would find it in about four years in about nine seconds, and everything the printer has done would be done twice and the second time it would be this yard's fault. **SO: NO REPLY. AND THE REASON GOES IN THE FIRST COLUMN, because a man in this harbor can come up this hill and look at that acknowledgment and see the name of an office at the foot of it and no man's name on it anywhere, and can ask a woman of fifty-eight whether she read it out and in what market and on what day, and can ask the post what day a letter came up this road. All three are checkable by any man who can get off a mole, and he is not going to argue with it.** The shape of sentence is said once, about a copy of a record in this yard's hands and this yard's name on a line in a printer's book.

### Chapter 210 — Three Weeks Read Out at the Head of This Dock, and a Day Refused
Thursday the twenty-fourth and Friday the twenty-fifth of December, at the head of this dock, and in the shed. **The cistern is charged on the rota day and walked on none, and the far bench is not occupied on either of the two days at any hour.** At about half past four on the Thursday, at the head of this dock in about eleven people's hearing, the three weeks are read out in about nine minutes, **and not one line of the second column is read out in it**: the bag at seven marks, six shillings and four pence, being one thousand one hundred and ninety-six pence, and the whole of the difference since the sixth of December a day at the second seam and a sheet of paper at a penny, **and a day refused at about three marks on the Friday and that figure is not in the bag and it is the first day in four volumes that this yard has turned money down**; the column at twenty-six lines and one hundred and four marks with the fifth of March the oldest, twenty envelopes, and nine weeks fallen due with no envelope against them given in the same breath, **two of those not paid because there was a sheet of paper and a bone and seven of them not paid because there was nothing**; every debt standing; **the wall bare three times, being the thirty-first, thirty-second and thirty-third Mondays since the first of May, and no sheet gone up on any wall in this harbor in seven weeks and none come off**; the cistern charged on six rota days and walked on none and the charge of the ninth of November standing with no figure against it; the far bench occupied on four days, the seventh, the eleventh, the eighteenth and the twenty-first, and on no other, and neither brother going over on any of the four; **two men of the first lay a morning down on a lighter, two marks and a victual of it paid on the Saturday evening for the Monday, the price entered on the Sunday and not asked for**; **and those same two men saying a thing about this yard at the head of that dock on the seventeenth and again on the nineteenth, the word *hold* twice on the first, about nine people standing there, the yard going up on neither day, the first of the two out of its own eyes and the second out of a man's mouth at the boards, and the reason against both lines that a correction is the reason and the reason does not go into a street**; **the price of a checkable page in nine places and the price of an uncheckable one, both out of a woman's mouth at a counter and not paid for except a penny for a sheet**; and **five papers that none of them can check, being the page and a half, the acknowledgment, the form of the court, the form with no date and no signature, and the printer's letter with one question in it that is going into a drawer and is not going to be answered.** On the Friday morning a man of the second lay comes about a day at about three marks for the Saturday **and the holder says no, in a doorway and not in a market, and gives the reason: a day at a seam is a figure that is earned, and this yard is a party of one and a half with nine weeks of no envelope against it, and it is not turning down three marks in the last week of December because a man came up a hill with a sheet of paper; and the second half of the reason is that he does not know what he is doing on the Saturday morning, and a man who books a day he does not know about books a day with a man in it twice, and this yard is already a day short.** The figure of three marks is entered against that day and not against nothing. The read-back runs, and then: **the turn, in the holder's own left hand, with the date and the hour on it, in the first column — a yard which has spent four volumes saying that a record a man can check is the only kind worth having has found out in three weeks that being checkable is being findable, and that the instrument it built to make itself credible is the instrument that is going to make it locatable, and that it built that instrument on purpose on the seventh of July last and nobody made it do it. It is in the first column and not the second because a man in this harbor can come up this hill on any day of a week and check it: he can ask a woman of fifty-eight what she copied and for how much and on whose account, he can go and count the sheets on a wall in this market, and he can find out whether this yard has said a thing out loud in the open in this harbor this month, and it has said one thing about the answer to that sheet, at the head of that dock on the Wednesday the sixteenth, in about eleven people's hearing, and it did not take it back and is not going to, and the tenth of the month was a question asked and an answer given and is not counted.** The new-volume question is referred to once, in the first column, with its date, its asker, his age and his tonnage, **with the yard's answer in the words already on the page and not improved on, and the entry saying it is not going to answer it in a book either.** The batch's last line is a fact: the bag, the three weeks, the refused day with its figure entered, a sheet with an office's name at the foot of it that has gone nine days from a market in this harbor to a line in a printer's book, and a letter going into a drawer unanswered, **and that every part of it is on that page and anybody in this town can come up this hill on any day of a week and look at it.**

### Chapter 211 — A Board With Nothing in It For Fourteen Days, and the Thing After the Gland
Saturday the twenty-sixth and Sunday the twenty-seventh of December, in the shed, and in the engine house. **No day of work booked on either day, and the reason against the line is that a day on which nothing happens still happened.** The far bench is entered as not occupied on both days, with no figure of what came before it, and with the reason that there are days of this month the yard has not entered and is not going up that hill again to enter. **The compressor is looked at for the first time since the twentieth of October and the shipwright asks what *useless* means, and the answer is the chapter: the part is on the machine and it holds and the pressure does not come out anywhere, and there is no hose in this harbor that is not the fire party's, and the fire party's is dead until the spring. SHE ASKS HIM TO WRITE DOWN WHAT A FOOT OF HOSE COSTS AND HE SAYS NO, and gives the reason, that he does not know and is not going to get the figure out of a shop, and that the answer is not a figure: a compressor in a harbor with no hose is a thing that makes noise.** She writes no figure and writes *engine house* and *looked at* in her own hand. **The working question is who is going to hire it, and the answer is on the page: a day at a seam is a pump, a line, a man on the pump and a slack water, and this yard has the line and two of them and a writ and has not got a pump, and the harbor's gear has no boat of its own, and every day taken in three weeks came up the hill because a man brought a pump up it, and this party has not gone down the hill and asked anybody for a day.** The decision: **the yard will take the next day that comes up the hill**, and the reason is that a yard which is findable has to be seen working and there is no way to be findable and be quiet about it. The read-back runs. **No money in and nothing out on either day; 1,196p in the bag, 556p short of the wage, nine weeks fallen due with no envelope standing against them.**

### Chapter 212 — The Thirty-Fourth Monday, and a Day That Came In While Four Marks Did Not Go Out
Monday the twenty-eighth and Tuesday the twenty-ninth of December, on the mole's steps, on the face of the second seam, and in the shed. **The wall comes down bare for the thirty-fourth Monday since the first of May, the counter does not restart, the clerk keeps his own page, and no sitting is held. The cistern is charged on the rota day and walked on none.** At ten at the foot of the mole's steps a man of the second lay with a boat of about seventy tons asks about the Tuesday and **is told yes**, and the reason is given in a doorway and not in a market: **on the Friday the holder could not say what he was doing on the Saturday morning and on the Monday he can say, and that is the whole of the change and it is not humility.** The man had a reason for coming back on the Friday morning and says it was the wrong one. The day is worked from eight until half past one, nobody in the water, a hand lead's line over the side and back five times, the board twelve gross and nine paid at the mole and the yard's three, and the board's own device runs at half past one in the daylight. **On the Tuesday afternoon a wage of four marks did not go in and no envelope was made and the number of weeks fallen due is TEN, all ten in the same breath. THREE MARKS CAME IN THAT MORNING AND FOUR MARKS DID NOT GO OUT THAT AFTERNOON, ON THE SAME COLUMN, ON THE SAME DAY, AND THE BAG IS TEN MARKS, SIX SHILLINGS AND FOUR PENCE, BEING ONE THOUSAND SIX HUNDRED AND SEVENTY-SIX PENCE, AND THE FIGURE IT IS SHORT OF THE WAGE IS ONE THOUSAND AND THIRTY-SIX PENCE WHERE IT WAS FIVE HUNDRED AND FIFTY-SIX PENCE THAT MORNING. THE REASON IS ENTERED HERE, ONCE, IN THE HOLDER'S OWN LEFT HAND, IN THE FIRST COLUMN: a wage is a week and not a payment, one week out of ten leaves a bag that pays one week and no other, a man paid in the last week of December and nothing in the second week of January has been given a figure and not a wage, this yard has a page for a figure and does not want one, and a column with the fifth of March still the oldest and the twenty-ninth of December newest is nine weeks out of order and this yard is not going to begin it.**

### Chapter 213 — The List of What a Thing on the Water Is Made Of, and a Year Nobody Noticed
Wednesday the thirtieth and Thursday the thirty-first of December, in the shed, and on the crown of the mole. **The holder writes the cost of that day against that day, in his own hand, in the first column, before the day is spent, and gives the reason: the day is going to be spent whether they agree to a thing at the end of it or not, and a day agreed to and not spent would be a lie with a date on it.** They go up the crown, stand at the two objects of nobody's, touch neither, and look at the mouth of the harbor for four minutes. **THE SHIPWRIGHT WRITES NINETEEN LINES ON THE BOARDS WITH HER BACK TO THE WIND AND READS THEM OUT ONCE, AND EVERY ITEM IS A FIGURE THIS YARD CANNOT PAY OR GEAR IT DOES NOT OWN: a body of iron, a chain and not rope, a weight, something to be seen from a boat in the dark, a mark and not a name, a place to put it that is not a berth and not somebody's room, and a hand that goes out and looks at it. THE SEVENTH IS THE ONE THAT STOPS HER AND SHE WROTE IT LAST ON PURPOSE, and a hand is a person and a person is not a figure and is not going on that page. THE ONLY OTHER NAME THIS YARD HAS IS NOT ON THE LIST, and if his name went on a thing in the water it would go on a thing that owes him more than any other thing in this harbor, and no man alive is going to put a boy's name on a buoy to make a list look finished. A LIST IS NOT A PLAN; A PLAN IS WHAT YOU DO WHEN SOMEBODY IS GOING TO PAY FOR IT.** The cistern is charged on the Thursday and walked on none. **No agreement is made and the reason is entered: not one of the nineteen is a thing this yard can pay for, not one is gear it owns, and the seventh is a person, and this yard has not got a person and is not writing that down in a book that goes in two columns because it can be checked every morning by counting.** The day's three marks is entered as the cost of a day spent and not as a price of anything. **On the Thursday afternoon at the head of this dock a man says out loud that this was the last day of the year and that a man ought to be able to say what he had done in it, and neither of them answers him, and neither of them says the day of the month, and that is entered because a day on which a thing is said in a street is a day on which a thing was said in a street. THE BATCH CROSSES A YEAR BOUNDARY HERE AND IN CHAPTER 215 AND NOT ONE CHARACTER NOTICES.** **Item eleven is said out loud in this yard's own book as a thing this yard cannot put in front of anybody, and it is not off the list and is not going to be.** No buoy went into any water and no stage was taken.

### Chapter 214 — The Berth Nobody in That Crew Has Gone to See, and a Share With a Figure Missing Off It
Friday the first and Saturday the second of January, in the shed, at the boards, and at the foot of the mole's steps. **No day of work booked on either day, and the Friday is a day on which a day of work was offered and not taken, and those are two things and the second of them is a cost.** **NO DAY OF THIS CHAPTER IS A SCENE IN THE BERTH. The holder gets up the mole's steps twice and sits down on the third of them for about a minute and a half, and the reason is in the second column with no name in it, and the day offered for the Saturday is refused by the body, and the figure of three marks is entered against that Saturday and against no day at all, and the man of the second lay was not told a figure had been put against it.** At nine at the boards a man of the third lay, about forty, one of three part owners in a boat of about forty tons, tells the ship's hand and not the holder that his boat has been a hand short since the fifteenth of May and that the boy was a hand on it in the last week of June and was paid the same as the man he took. **THE COST: the boat has been short since the middle of July and two nights short twice, the three part owners have been making it up out of the shares, and FOUR MARKS, ONE SHILLING AND EIGHTPENCE, BEING SIX HUNDRED AND SIXTY PENCE, HAS COME OUT OF HIS OWN SHARE, and it is not coming back and nobody is going to ask this yard for it, and she entered that the figure came out of a share and not out of a day's work and those are not the same thing and are not going to be the same thing in this book.** He asked for nothing and said he would not ask in a month either. **AND THEN HE SAID THE THING SHE WAS NOT GOING TO ASK HIM: there are four men in that crew now and not one of them has gone to see the boy who was in that boat in June, and he is one of the four and has not gone, and he said it because the yard was going to hear it said that a crew went to look after its own and nobody went.** The yard has not gone either and the difference is three letters and the man noticed it. **The three nots stand, forty marks stands whole, the third column keeps the boy's hand, and the yard did not go, did not write, did not send anybody, did not ask anybody who has seen him what he said, and entered that with the reason of the fourth of October past, not a new one, not invented again.** The read-back is absent and the chapter says why.

### Chapter 215 — The Thirty-Fifth Monday, and a Date on a Half Sheet That Arrived and Nothing Happened
Sunday the third and Monday the fourth of January, in the shed, and on the mole. **The wall comes down bare for the thirty-fifth Monday and the cistern is charged and walked on none, and the far bench is entered as not occupied on both days with no figure of what came before it.** On the Sunday morning the ship's hand says the only thing anybody in that shed says about the month, which is that the month is out, and the holder says the month being out is the whole of the news in that room, and she says that is all she has got and does not get past it, and **the entry says a thing said in a shed is a thing that happened, and that neither of them noticed anything else.** The read-back runs. **AND ON MONDAY THE FOURTH THE DATE ON THE HALF SHEET ARRIVED AND NOTHING HAPPENED, AND A YARD WHICH HAS SPENT FOUR VOLUMES WRITING DOWN EVERYTHING THAT HAPPENS TO IT WRITES DOWN THAT, IN THOSE WORDS, WITH THE DAY ON IT, AND DOES NOT IMPROVE ON IT. THE YARD DID NOT PRODUCE THE COPY. NO COURT IN THIS HARBOR SAT. NO CLERK OF THAT COURT CAME UP THAT HILL. NOBODY WAS CHARGED WITH ANYTHING. NOBODY WAS FINED, BECAUSE A COURT DOES NOT FINE A POOR MAN AND THE MATTER SIMPLY GOES ON. NOBODY WAS REFUSED ANYTHING, BECAUSE THAT COURT FINDS AGAINST NOBODY. NOBODY WAS EXAMINED.** The reason is the reason of the seventh of December past, said out loud in a shed once. **And the half of it that is in the shed and not in a market: he could produce that copy tomorrow, it is nine feet away in a drawer, a letter would be up the road in four days and in a man's hand in nine and in a court in ten, and nothing whatever would happen to anybody, and in about four years a clerk would have a piece of paper with this yard's name at the head of it and a date on it.** The writ runs to the first of March, four names and no fifth, not the Second Bell license; the yard is a party of one and a half until that day and there are fifty-five days between. **No money in and nothing out on either day; 1,676p in the bag.**

### Chapter 216 — A Page Off a Press in a City, and a Man's Own Figure Refused
Tuesday the fifth and Wednesday the sixth of January, in the shed, and at the head of this dock. **The wage does not go in, no envelope is made, and the number of weeks fallen due is ELEVEN, all eleven in the same breath, and the reason is the reason of the twenty-ninth of December and is not improved on.** **On the Wednesday morning a page is lying on the boards in this harbor with a stone on it, put there to keep a slate flat, and it is a page off a printed sheet out of a press in a city, folded twice, with a thumb's width of dirt in the second fold, and it has been on those boards since the Saturday before last and the man whose stone it was was in a boat. IT CARRIES TWO FIGURES FOR THE BAR OF GRAVEL OFF THE MOUTH OF THIS HARBOR SIDE BY SIDE IN THE SAME WEIGHT OF TYPE, being two and three-quarters and two and a half, and the second is a pilot's figure and the first is not, and nobody in this yard wrote either of them. NEITHER IS WITHDRAWN, BOTH ARE IN TYPE, THIS VOLUME DOES NOT DECIDE WHICH OF THEM IS RIGHT, THE KETTLE WAS NOT LOOKED AT, AND THE REFUSED LICENCE STANDS UNREVERSED AND THE THIRTY MARKS WERE NOT PAID AND ARE NOT OWED TO ANYBODY AND CANNOT BE APPLIED TO ANYTHING.** A man of fifty-eight of the first lay with a boat of about two hundred and thirty tons, who has taken that printed sheet out of a city every three weeks for nineteen years, comes up the hill and says first that he did not know the figure was this yard's and that he put a stone on it and did not read it and did not keep it and did not send it anywhere, and asks for that to be in the book before anything else. **He offers his own figure for that bar, a mark and a half, in his own book since he was thirty-four, with a year and a month on it and the name of a man who had been in his boat. THE HOLDER REFUSES IT IN THE STREET, SAYS HE IS NOT ASKING FOR THE FIGURE AND IS REFUSING ONE, AND GIVES ITEM ELEVEN AS THE REASON, and says that a thing said out loud in a book has two people in it and a thing said out loud in a street has about eleven, and he would rather the two were right than the eleven heard it; and that a book that has this yard's figures in it is a book that goes somewhere, and the day it has the man's figure in it beside theirs is the day it is a book of opinions about a bar, and that page is already on a list and has been since the twenty-second of November and is not coming off, and if it came off that list it would come off in a book, in this yard's own hand, on a Monday, and that is the only instrument this yard has.** The man says it is a straight answer, then says that he has been over that bar forty-one times and that **there is not one man on this coast who has ever wanted his figure, and that he has told it to two people in thirty years and one of them is dead.** **THE COST OF THE CHAPTER: THIS YARD'S OWN METHOD WENT OUT LOUD IN A STREET IN ABOUT NINE PEOPLE'S HEARING FOR THE FIRST TIME IN FOUR VOLUMES, and what went out loud was the method and not a figure.** 1,676p in the bag.

### Chapter 217 — A Hand at a Counter, and What a Yard of One and a Half Can Actually Build
Thursday the seventh and Friday the eighth of January, in a room off the fish market, on the face of the second seam, and in the shed. **The cistern is charged on the Thursday and walked on none.** The shipwright puts nineteen lines on the copyist's counter. **THE QUESTION PUT TO THE TRADE IS NOT ABOUT PAPER: what a yard of one and a half with about three marks in it can actually build, what a man has got to know to keep a thing on the water, and how many days a year a thing on the water takes out of a working harbor.** **A COPYIST PRICES WHAT SHE CAN PRICE AND SAYS PLAINLY WHAT SHE CANNOT, AND GIVES THE REASON: a guess in a list is worse than a blank, because a blank can be filled in by a man who knows and a guess cannot be got out.** Three things, two in her trade and one not, and she says which is which: **a yard of one and a half with about three marks in it cannot build anything, and that is a thing about three marks and not a thing about anybody; a thing on the water is not a building, a building is made once and a thing on the water is a bill, and it is a bill every time anybody looks at it, and nobody can produce the number of times anybody is going to look at it, and a man who gives you that number has made it up; and in thirty-one years no man who ever came to that counter with a list has asked her what it will cost to BE the thing afterwards.** A man has got to know when it was last looked at, by whom and what it was found to be, and all three of those go in a book, and a book costs nothing to keep, **and that is exactly why it is not worth anything.** There is nobody in this harbor she would send a boat out in with in February. **SHE CHARGES NOTHING, SHE SOLD NOTHING, AND NO MONEY OF THIS WARD WENT INTO THAT ROOM. She was not told what a second column is, she did not ask, and the reason she gives is that he had been showing her things for about an hour and had not sold her one, and she did not ask about a thing she had not been given.** On the Friday the yard works, the man of the second lay brings the pump and a man, and **the compressor is not used and could not have been, the gland holds and nothing came out of the other end of it, and so the day came on a pump a man brought up a hill, and the three marks is for the hands and the line and the tide, and not one penny of it is for a machine — and that is what a day at a seam in this harbor is.** A second-column fact with a reason against it and no name in it. **2,156p in the bag.**

### Chapter 218 — A Man With a Firebox and a Key, and a No That Was Read as Something Else
Saturday the ninth and Sunday the tenth of January, at the head of this dock, and in the shed. **No day of work booked on either day, and the Saturday is a day on which this yard was asked a question in the open in about eleven people's hearing and gave an answer in the open, and those are two things and neither of them is work.** **THE PARTY IS ASKED, REASONABLY AND IN GOOD FAITH, TO HAND ITS ONE TRUE RECORD TO A PLACE THAT WILL KEEP IT SAFE.** A man of sixty-eight has kept a dry room with a firebox, a lock and a shelf above a chandler's on the north side of this town since he was thirty-one, and has kept other people's things in it, and has never charged anybody for the shelf and does not know that that is unusual. **He says he is not asking for anything; that he thought about it for three weeks; that he is sixty-eight and has a dry floor and a firebox and a key; that he is not going to be responsible for a piece of paper in the spring, because the room goes to a man who buys chandlers and will put it in a sack; that he is not asking this yard to sign anything, is not asking what is on it, is not asking it to be examined about it, does not know what that means and does not want to know; and is asking for one shelf and a key and would give a second key this yard will not need. THE ANSWER IS NO, IN THE STREET, WITH THE REASON GIVEN IN THE STREET, AND THE PRICE OF SAYING IT IS NAMED BEFORE IT IS SAID: that about eleven people are hearing the reason.** The reason: **a shelf in that man's room is not a place to keep a record, it is a favor, and a favor is a thing with a man at the end of it, and the whole of what this yard has said for four volumes is that a thing with a man at the end of it can be got at by anybody who can be pleasant to that man.** The man says that is fair and that he would not have said it so tidy. **And the holder says one more thing in the street because if he does not say it now it will be said about him in a month: this yard has not got anywhere to put it, and there is no such place in this harbor, and this yard is not going to make one in a fortnight.** The man says he will not ask again and will tell people that he asked and was told no, and will not say this yard is keeping it because he did not see it keeping it. **AND THEN THE NO IS READ AS AN INTENTION TO KEEP: two men of the first lay who were standing there, neither of them asked anything and neither of them ever to be, say that a dry room was turned down in this town in this weather, and that they will have somewhere, and that a man does not turn down a man's key unless he has a better one.** The yard cannot correct it without putting a name on something. **The three sentences a man can say in a street are given and every one is shown to be wrong, and the reason it is not the four of the Sill is said out loud in the shed — because saying that would put four people in a street — and the reason is that the only way to be read correctly is to have somewhere to put it that is not a hold, and this yard has not got one.** The shape of sentence is said once. **THE GAP IS NOT CLOSED AND THE CHAPTER SAYS SO ON THE PAGE: the answer is a hole and a hole in a street fills itself.** No chain of custody is described. **2,156p in the bag.**

### Chapter 219 — The Thirty-Sixth Monday, and a Name Taken Off a List With a Pen
Monday the eleventh and Tuesday the twelfth of January, on the mole, at the boards, and in the shed. **The wall comes down bare for the thirty-sixth Monday, the cistern is charged and walked on none, and the far bench is occupied from about ten until about four and neither brother goes over, and he was not at it on the Sunday at any hour.** The yard works, and the pump and the man come up the hill from the same man as always. **THE COST HAS A SHAPE OF ITS OWN AND IT IS NOT ANY OF THE FIVE THE BATCH WAS FORBIDDEN: at about two in the afternoon a man of the second lay tells the ship's hand and not the holder that a man of thirty-one in that crew had put his own name down at a yard in the next town down this coast on the Wednesday, for the Monday and the Tuesday, because that yard had a week of work and wanted two men; that the man of the second lay hired him on the Thursday and told him on the Thursday; and that on the Friday afternoon the man of thirty-one took his own name off that list and a woman in that yard's office wrote it off a list in a book with a pen in about four seconds, and the figure of it is about six marks and he will not get it back. A MAN IS NOT ASKED AT THAT PLACE AGAIN, AND THAT IS NOT A DAY AND IT IS NOT A WAGE AND IT CANNOT BE PUT BACK BY ANYBODY. A COLUMN NOBODY FILLS IS A COLUMN SOMEBODY ELSE FILLS, AND THE SOMEBODY ELSE WAS A WOMAN WITH A PEN IN ANOTHER TOWN AND ABOUT FOUR SECONDS, AND SHE DID NOT KNOW WHAT SHE WAS WRITING OFF AND COULD NOT BE ASKED ABOUT IT.** **The name is in the speaker's mouth and is not in this yard's book, and the reason is entered against the line: a yard that puts a name in its own book on the day the name comes off another man's book has turned a man's list into a page, and that is the whole of what a page is for.** On the Tuesday the wage does not go in, no envelope is made, and the number of weeks fallen due is TWELVE, all twelve in the same breath, and the reason is the reason of the twenty-ninth of December and is not improved on, and **the bag is short of the wage by one thousand nine hundred and ninety-six pence where it was one thousand and thirty-six a week before, because three marks came in on the Monday and the figure got worse.** 2,636p in the bag.

### Chapter 220 — A Corner Gone Soft on a Bench, and There Is No Place in This Harbor That Is Not a Hold
Wednesday the thirteenth and Thursday the fourteenth of January, on the crown of the mole, and in the shed. **The cistern is charged on the Thursday and walked on none, and that is the sixth rota day walked on none since the twenty-eighth of December and the tenth since the third of August. The far bench is entered as not occupied on both days with no figure of what came before it.** They go up the crown, go past the two objects of nobody's, touch neither, stand about two minutes and come down. **AND ON THE THURSDAY MORNING THE SHIPWRIGHT PICKS UP THE SHEET AND A HALF WITH NO SIGNATURE AND NO DATE ON IT, WHICH HAS BEEN ON THAT BENCH SINCE THE NINTH OF DECEMBER PAST UNDER A PUMP, AND A CORNER OF IT IS GONE SOFT ABOUT THE WIDTH OF TWO FINGERS AND IS NOT WET. SHE SAYS THAT THAT IS A MONTH, that she has known since the fourteenth of December past that she was not going to write that sentence this month and has written nothing since and has not once lifted it off that bench, and the reason she has not lifted it off that bench is that there is nowhere in this harbor for her to lift it on to. THE SHEET IS GOING. IT CAME UP THAT HILL ON FOOT AND IT IS GOING SOFT ON A BENCH UNDER A PUMP, AND IT IS A SHEET AND A HALF AND IT IS WORTH A PENNY AND THERE IS NOT A FIGURE OF A PENNY AGAINST IT AND A MAN CANNOT BUY IT ANYWHERE ON THIS COAST.** **The cost of the three weeks is read out in one breath and every item in it is a figure on the page: a day of about three marks refused by this yard, a day of about three marks not taken, four marks one shilling and eightpence out of a man's share, this yard's own method said out loud in a street, a name off a list in another town, about six marks a man will not get back — and the last of those six items is a sheet of paper.** The read-back runs. **And then the turn goes in, in the first column, in the holder's own left hand, with the date and the hour on it: that a party which has refused to hold a copy of a record for the coast, and has said so in the open in about eleven people's hearing, is read as a party which means to keep it; and that the only way to be read correctly is to have somewhere to put it that is not a hold; and that this yard has not got one, and knows what one would cost, and is not going to pretend it knows the cost of a thing it has not built.** It is in the first column because a man in this town can come up that hill on any day of a week and ask whether they have got a place to put a page in and be told they have not, and can go down to a chandler's and ask a man of sixty-eight. **A QUESTION IS LEFT STANDING THAT THIS BATCH DOES NOT ANSWER — what a place that is not a hold is, and who would keep it, and what it costs, and where the money comes from in a harbor where the mole-head light is not on because a light costs oil — and the entry says this party is not going to answer it in a market, in a room, or in this book.** Nobody in the shed argued with the third entry and nobody improved on it. **The batch's last line is a fact and is checkable by any man who can get off a mole: the bag, the cistern walked on, a corner gone soft on a bench, and that there is no place in this harbor that is not a hold.**
