THE BATCH RUNS WEDNESDAY THE FIFTH OF MAY TO FRIDAY THE FOURTEENTH OF MAY, and the departure from lock L1 item 5 is the same recorded departure and is not re-argued here.**

**THE THREE STANDING FIGURES WERE COUNTED OFF THE ANCHOR AND NOT TAKEN FROM ANY STATE FILE, AND THE HAND-OFF THAT DISPATCHED THIS BATCH GOT ALL THREE WRONG AND SAID SO: it counted THREE Mondays, FOUR rota days and TWO wage Tuesdays. THERE IS ONE MONDAY IN THE SPAN, BEING MONDAY THE TENTH; THERE ARE THREE CISTERN ROTA DAYS, BEING THURSDAY THE SIXTH, MONDAY THE TENTH AND THURSDAY THE THIRTEENTH; AND THERE IS ONE WAGE TUESDAY, BEING TUESDAY THE ELEVENTH.**

### Chapter 291 — *Wednesday the Fifth of May, Six Hundred and Forty-Nine Pence In, a Man of Sixty-One With a Boat, and a Crown Seal Named Out Loud in a Shed*

**Wednesday the fifth of May. The shed on the mole, and the water off the face of the second seam in the morning.** **SIX HUNDRED AND FORTY-NINE PENCE COMES IN AND NOTHING GOES OUT, being three marks for a day's work at the standing rate, one mark and eightpence for a boat and a man in her, and a penny of line, on a private person's account and for a private person's purpose, not salvage and not a claim and not work under any instrument, and the party was never in the water.** It is the first money into that yard since the eleven marks of Elias Voke on the sixth of April, and it makes the whole of what has come in since the sixth of December two thousand four hundred and nine pence. **And the hire is closed inside itself: a man of about fifty-five states the question out loud before he is asked — whether the thing along his boat's starboard side is his or was there before he bought her — and gets his answer on the page. They go down in a hired pan at about ten and get a line to the ground, and there is a cut in the rock about nine feet long and about two inches across, with dressed sides and square at both ends, counted twice and got nine feet twice; she tells him it is not his, because a thing a man puts into the ground is not square at both ends, and that it was cut before she was built and will be there after she is sold, and that she cannot tell him whose it is and neither can anybody in that harbor; it goes in the first column because a man can go and look at it with a pan and a line and not because anybody knows what it is. He puts the whole of it on the bench without arguing with a figure, says he had not expected to be told anything at all, and does not ask what it meant and does not ask to have his boat up again.** Then **a retired salvage foreman of sixty-one, half deaf, with a boat of about nineteen tons that is not the yard's and a hand of about forty he cannot do without, comes up the steps unasked and is asked for the boat and for nothing else, and sets one condition: his boat goes and he takes her and nobody stands in her but him at the wheel.** He says out loud that he has seen the chamber's name cut in the cheek of a stair more than sixty years ago, is asked nothing further, and **NAMES THE CROWN SEAL FOR THE FIRST TIME IN VOLUME 06 IN A CHARACTER'S MOUTH: a round of glass the size of a saucer in a metal collar with a wheel in the collar and a screw through it, sitting in a bed cut in a sill, the screw taking a load off a thing made of the same glass, the mark of its name cut into the round — a machine and a seal at once — and a term and not a lock, because whoever cut it wanted a thing held for so long and no longer and cut the figure on the sill so the next man would know when, and nobody in this country ever looks at one.** The yard does not enter that word in the first column, and the reason is that a man's memory of a thing he saw a long time ago is a thing a man can go and look at exactly once. **Then the price of a descent to be made on the Thursday is named out loud before anybody agrees to it at fourteen marks, three shillings and fourpence, component for component, the same four figures the eighth, ninth, tenth and twenty-eighth of April came to, and THE YARD CANNOT PAY IT: there are five marks, one shilling and fourpence in the drawer.** A day's set goes off a book at a pressure house at Ternwatch because **a man of sixty-one asked for it**, and the holder of that writ says out loud, before agreeing, that **he has a rule about a man this yard has paid and has not got a rule about a man this yard is being paid by.** And last, the decision, taken in advance, on a Wednesday at four o'clock, before anybody has agreed to anything: **if there is a chamber down there and if it is open enough to be read, this yard will not read it and will not take one piece of it out, and the price of that is named in the same breath — the only proof this yard will ever have of anything about a drowned city will be in a thing that closed, and a proof nobody can check is worth less than nothing.** Cost: six hundred and forty-nine pence in, nothing out.

### Chapter 292 — *Thursday the Sixth of May, the Forty-Fourth Charge Walked on None, Fourteen Marks Three Shillings and Fourpence Off a Book That Is Not This Yard's, and a Doorway at Seventy Fathoms*

**Thursday the sixth of May. Off the south end of that mole, and the shed in the evening.** **THE CISTERN IS CHARGED AND WALKED ON NONE, BEING THE FORTY-FOURTH.** The set is on the mole at six. **The book sends Amos Searle and the yard says no out loud, and the reason is that book on the ninth and the twenty-eighth of April and is entered as a book and not as care of a man, and the second name on it is sent at the same figure, two marks and eightpence a day.** The six conditions are read at the boat, the bar is named in the same words since the seventh of April. **The pot is down at about twenty past eight and up at about half past eleven, about three hours and ten minutes, and the gauge reads eighty-four pounds on a face of a hundred and fifty and the pencil mark is not moved.** **AT ABOUT SEVENTY FATHOMS THE GROUND IS CUT AS A FACE OF THIRTY FEET AND THERE IS A DOORWAY IN IT, five feet of dressed jambs and one dressed stone across the top, and a room about nine feet across, and a sill with a bed cut its whole length and, under the lip of the sill, a second groove, deeper and wider, which is a rebate and not a bed; and a plate of glass about the width of the doorway and a hand and a half high stands in the bed and not in the rebate, and the bed is empty.** Nobody puts a hand in and nobody goes in, and the not-going is said out loud in the boat so that it is a record and not a story. **In the inner face of the sill to the left of the bed there is a row of two hundred and nine marks, cut in the same hand as the figures at the near end of every course on that mole's own floor, with one further cut below the row, deeper and not the same cut, and the row was counted three times and all three counts were the same; and beside the row there is a day cut in the same hand and the same stroke, being the eleventh of May.** Both figures go in the first column because a man in a boat can go and look at them and not because anybody knows what they mean. **He is on the crown of that mole from one until five and loses the afternoon; there is no figure for an afternoon and the reason against the line has no name in it; and he writes the entry himself in his own right hand, slowly, in about twenty minutes, and nobody helps him.** Cost: nothing in, nothing out. Fourteen marks three shillings and fourpence went off a book four hundred miles off.

### Chapter 293 — *Friday the Seventh of May, a Far Bench Occupied From Ten Until Four, a Round of Glass Out of a Bed, and About Six Hours of Weather in That Harbor*

**Friday the seventh of May. Off the south end, and the shed in the evening.** **THE FAR BENCH IS OCCUPIED FROM TEN UNTIL FOUR AND IS NOT APPROACHED.** The reason for going out again is said out loud at eight in the morning before anybody is asked: **the plate stands in the bed, the bed is empty, and on the eleventh the plate goes into the rebate, and a plate the width of a doorway does not come down on a stone floor and stay off it — so the round comes up, or the plate grinds it into about nine pieces in a room at seventy fathoms and nobody in this country ever hears of it again.** The man of about thirty-four reads the price off the paper and adds that **the round goes on the crown of that mole and does not go into that shed, because the second clause of the notice of the thirty-first of October keeps recovered transparent mineral out of every shed in this harbor, and this yard did not write that clause and cannot lift it.** The man off the Crown house book goes down. **What comes up is a round of glass the size of a saucer in a metal collar with a wheel in the collar and a screw down about two turns and stopped, and on the collar a chain of eleven links about a finger long, every link stamped and a date stamped on every link, the last link thicker than the others with something struck on its face under the shadow of the collar which nobody scratched at to read; and under the round a piece of worked glass the size of a hand that will not come away from it and goes up with it.** The crate goes on the crown under a tarpaulin and not into the shed. **Then the weather comes up out of the south-west at about four in the afternoon and does not stop until about ten that night, and no cause is named for it in any column of any book.** The skylight, the door light, a pane of plate out of a shopfront, the running glass of two boats, eleven windows and the lens of the mole-head light all go, and about a hundred people are out in the rain and nobody is hurt. **The storm is entered in two hands on two pages that are not on top of each other**, and the second hand is not the ship's and the second page is not in that shed and is in a man's own book four streets off the front of that harbor, the two pages being a mile and a quarter apart. Cost: nothing in, nothing out.

### Chapter 294 — *Saturday the Eighth of May, a Pane of Plate Glass on a Mole, a Mark and Four Shillings to a Bonesetter, and Until the Second of June*

**Saturday the eighth of May. The crown of that mole, a room over a chandler's, and the shed in the evening.** **TWO HUNDRED AND EIGHT PENCE GOES OUT TO A BONESETTER.** The injury in three parts. **The hand:** a pane of plate about four feet by three, lying on the mole stones since the evening before, and two men lifting it and the wind coming round the head of the mole and putting it over on the crown across the right hand of a man of sixty-one — **the right hand, and the left shoulder and ribs belong to a man of twenty-eight in a bed at Ashtide until the fifth of June and are not this**. **The bill:** a bonesetter of about sixty with thirty-one years and about four hundred things, who comes down the hill because he does not go up one, who says the palm and not the back and that the line along the little finger has not gone near the bone, and **who does not say no and who is not going to be asked twice and says he is not going to hear a word about who brought him** — a mark and four shillings, two hundred and eight pence, paid at a counter over a chandler's at about twenty past ten. **The absence and the consequence:** the hand will grip again by the middle of June and about two-thirds of what it gripped on Friday, and nine parts in ten by Christmas; **a man of sixty-one states the figure himself and will not be argued out of it — he will not be on the water until the second of June — and says that the job on the north bar on the nineteenth of May is not going to happen.** And **his boat of about nineteen tons, which he built himself fifty years ago or near it, has taken a stove on the Friday night in the weather, nobody saw it and nobody has said what caused it, and eleven pieces of her frame came out of her on the Saturday and are on that mole and are not going back in.** He is not thanked and says he is not going to be, and the yard enters that it has a rule about a man it has paid and has not got a rule about a man it has hurt. Cost: two hundred and eight pence out.

### Chapter 295 — *Sunday the Ninth of May, Four Marks and a Shilling to a Glazier, One Man's Own Book Four Streets Off, and the Figure the Bag Is Short of the Wage of Four Marks Coming Back*

**Sunday the ninth of May. The shed, and a room four streets off it.** **SIX HUNDRED AND SIXTY PENCE GOES OUT TO A GLAZIER, being two lights and a skylight, and that is the whole of the glass this yard owns in that harbor; the rest of the front is other people's and the yard paid none of it and was offered none of it.** A man of about nineteen out of the second lay put the running glass into the man of about fifty-five's own boat out of his own pocket for sixpence and nobody in that shed has been told his name and it is not going in that book. **Then the other page of the weather: a man of sixty-one brings his own book up the steps unasked and puts it on the bench, and it is the same account in his own hand in the house of his own boat, four streets off, and in about four years it is the only record in the country of what that front looked like on the seventh, and nobody asked him for it and he is not going to be thanked.** And then the figure that has had no subject since the seventh of April comes back with one: **the bag holds five hundred and ninety-seven pence, the wage of four marks is six hundred and forty, and the figure this bag is short of the wage of four marks is forty-three pence, being three shillings and sevenpence — IT CAME BACK ON THE PAGE AS A FIGURE WITH A SUBJECT AND THE CHAPTER SAYS IT CAME BACK AND NOT THAT ANYBODY IS GOING TO PAY IT.** Nobody is asked what they thought of the weather, and not the man of about fifty-five, and not the boy, and not the four people four days down that coast. Cost: six hundred and sixty pence out.

### Chapter 296 — *Monday the Tenth of May, the Fifty-Third Time That Wall Came Down, the Forty-Fifth Charge Walked on None, and a Thing Said Twice in a Room With Two People In It*

**Monday the tenth of May. A room in this harbor, and the shed in the evening.** **THE WALL CAME DOWN BARE FOR THE FIFTY-THIRD TIME AND THE CISTERN WAS CHARGED AND WALKED ON NONE, BEING THE FORTY-FIFTH.** **The concealment, told once and once only, in a room with two people in it and nobody else in the room and nobody in the passage and nobody sent.** She names the price before he starts: **say it twice.** The first time it was told was in a shed at half past ten at night in about nine seconds in a voice he told her he could not have repeated, and she has never once been able to use it, and **a thing relearned off a sheet in ninety seconds is a record and not a story.** The price of that is hers: in about four years there will be nothing in any record about tonight, and the only thing that will be true of it is that a man of thirty-two and a woman of thirty were in a room on the tenth of May, and she has decided she can live with that and he has not. **And then it is said twice, in his own voice, out loud, in a room, and the first time is: there is a mark on it; it is a sound, not a crack and not a number; it comes up out of the bottom of this harbor in a ground line's delay, three marks and a flick, in a call a boy on a quay used to answer him with when they were eleven; it has been known since the fourteenth of October; it is his brother's; it is Oren's; it is the reason he went into that shard on the thirteenth of October; it is in no record, in no office, in no book and not in any chart.** Four things she will not do: **it does not restore the standing of a certificate of method in a market, which was spent on the twenty-second of April and cannot be given back; her forty marks first after food are not reduced and not paid and were not paid by a thing said in a room; he is not going to be forgiven and did not ask; and she is not going to be able to do it again, and he has known that since the second of January and has said nothing for four months, and she is not going to give him the fifth thing.** **Neither of them calls it a reunion and neither of them is going to.** Cost: nothing in, nothing out.

### Chapter 297 — *Tuesday the Eleventh of May, the Twenty-Ninth Week With No Envelope, a Round of Glass Carried Down to a Boat and Not Put Back, and a Sheet of Glass at Seventy Fathoms Going Down Into a Rebate and Stopping There*

**Tuesday the eleventh of May. Off the south end, and the shed in the evening.** **THE WAGE TUESDAY: THE COLUMN DOES NOT MOVE, NO ENVELOPE IS MADE, AND TWENTY-NINE WEEKS HAVE NOW FALLEN DUE.** **THE FAR BENCH IS OCCUPIED FROM TEN UNTIL FOUR AND IS NOT APPROACHED.** **THE LETTING.** The crate comes off the crown at ten and the round comes out about two feet from the top of the mole steps at about eleven in front of about nine people, and a man of sixty-one with two stitches in his right hand carries it down three steps and hands it over. **The exchange is on the stone of the mole steps from about eleven until the boat goes out at about half past eleven, with the ship's hand asking for it from the boat, and nothing is written on the day.** The ship's hand makes the holder of that writ say the whole of it out loud so that it is a record and not a story: **on the Wednesday before last, at four o'clock, before anybody had agreed to a day, this yard said it would not read the chamber and would not take one piece of it out, and named the price; and on the Friday the round was taken out of the bed with the plate standing in it with nothing behind it, and the round was taken out because the plate is going into the rebate on the eleventh.** The woman of no office says *then you are going to put it back*. **He says no, and gives the reason, and puts it down on the stone of the mole steps: because putting it back holds the plate up and opens that room, and they would go in and read it, and the proof would be in a thing that closes, and a proof in a thing that closed is a proof nobody can check, and this yard has said in six movements out loud that such a proof is worth less than a record a man can look at.** A man of sixty-one says one thing about a term and is not asked and does not say what he thinks of it. **The sentence is: the chamber closes whether it is read or not, and we are letting it close.** And: **that is not a surrender, and it is not a proof, and nobody in this boat is going to say this yard was right about it, because it does not know that it was right about it, and what it costs and what it is worth are not the same figure and the second one is zero.** Then the pot goes on the top course at twenty past twelve, is lifted straight off, and comes up at a quarter past two, and the slate says **THE PLATE IS DOWN IN THE REBATE AND IT IS NOT COMING UP, AND NOTHING IS HOLDING IT UP.** And that nobody in the boat saw the beginning of it, and a man in a boat could see the end of it, and **that is what a figure cut in a sill is for** — the same sentence as **the whole of what has happened in about three hundred years on the coast of this country**. Cost: one mark and eightpence out.

### Chapter 298 — *Wednesday the Twelfth of May, a Lead and a Slate at Seventy Fathoms With No Name on the Slate, Eleven Stamped Links With a Date on Each of Them, and Nine Lines He Cannot Say Any More*

**Wednesday the twelfth of May. Off the south end in the morning, and the crown of that mole in the afternoon.** **A LEAD OF ABOUT FOURTEEN POUNDS MARKED AT EVERY FATHOM GOES OVER THE SIDE WITH A SLATE TIED TO THE LINE, AND THE SLATE HAS NO NAME ON IT, because nobody signs anything in that yard and nobody has since the third of April past.** The entry, in the ship's own left hand, is that the plate is in the rebate, the bed is empty, the round is out, and **there are two hundred and nine marks in the sill and a day in the sill, being the eleventh of May, and the day is the same day it was on the Thursday preceding and has not been cut again** — checkable by anybody with a lead and fourteen pounds, in about nine minutes. Then, alone on the crown at about one, he reads the eleven stamped links twice out loud and, **holding a flat piece of glass to the light so as not to scratch at it, reads the mark on the last link: *Sealed and entered at the Morrow circuit office by order of Magister Ilya Serr, senior Registry examiner.* He is in no room, on no mole, in no market and not in this harbor, was not sent for and was not asked anything, and the name is a line, and the name is not spoken out loud in that shed on the said day and is spoken exactly once in these ten chapters, by the ship's hand, on the fourteenth, reading a column back, and by nobody else.** A date struck on the collar is later than the strike on that link, and the collar is the work of the same office named on the seal line, and **the difference between the two is a difference in two made things and says nothing whatever about which of them is right, or why, or by whom.** And then, at half past two, the ship's hand asks him for the third line of the order and he cannot say one of the nine. **THE PERMANENT LOSS: he read them under a lamp on the twenty-ninth of April and cannot say one of them now, and the sheet is in a drawer in that shed, and she reads them out and he has them again in about ninety seconds, and a thing relearned off a sheet in ninety seconds is not a thing remembered. THE RECORD OUTLIVES THE MAN AND THE MAN DOES NOT OUTLIVE THE RECORD, AND THIS YARD HAS BEEN SAYING THAT FOR SIX MOVEMENTS AND HAS NOT ONCE BEEN TOLD IT WAS A RULE ABOUT RECORDS.** It goes in the second column with the twelfth of May against it and a reason against the line and no name in it, and there is no figure for it. Cost: nothing in, nothing out.

### Chapter 299 — *Thursday the Thirteenth of May, the Forty-Sixth Charge Walked on None, Two Marks on the Bottom Four Hundred Yards Apart, a Name Said Out Loud Once, and Three People Saying Three Things That Are All True*

**Thursday the thirteenth of May. The shed, and the water inside the harbor line in the morning.** **THE CISTERN IS CHARGED AND WALKED ON NONE, BEING THE FORTY-SIXTH, AND THE FAR BENCH IS OCCUPIED FROM TEN UNTIL FOUR AND IS NOT APPROACHED.** A boat is hired at a mark and eightpence on a private person's account, the man of fifty-one is not in her and was not asked and his ground is not in it, and nobody goes inside the bar. **THE TWO MARKS ARE FOUR HUNDRED YARDS APART ON THE BOTTOM OF THIS HARBOR'S OWN GROUND: this yard's own, fouled about four inches on the twelfth of March and about a foot and a half on the thirteenth of May; and one that carries a line and a shackle of a make nobody in this harbor makes, whose season of nine marks is not paid and is not going to be asked for.** A man of sixty-one goes in the boat unasked, stands on the thwart, looks at the second mark from about ten feet off the bottom for about four minutes, says **I have seen that make and I have not seen one in this harbor in twenty years and there are two things I could say about it and I am not going to say either of them, because a man of sixty-one does not get to be right about a thing he has only seen once.** And then, at about eleven with the boat lying still: **the name at the head of the third column is said out loud once, and it is Mara Venn, and it is not the boy's and it is not the holder of that writ's, and it had not been said out loud before and it is said once.** Then the three statements, about three different things, all three true, none improved on: **about the sill**, by the ship's hand — two hundred and nine marks and a day, a chamber was held and the holding had a term and no man read it, and nobody is going to write *because* anywhere near any of it; **about the round**, by the woman of no office — a machine that holds a room open for a term so that somebody has to go and look at it is the most sensible thing she has ever heard of, and they were right, and a reason is not a warrant, and this yard does not get to be the one that says that office was right; and the two figures, nine communities and six centuries, and three hundred years of the office that answers it, are not in one another's sentence; **about the room**, by the holder of that writ — nine communities' votes held in one room that could not all be true together, a hazard and not an archive, and the proof this yard has is physical and labor-based and about half of it is unreadable, and a man of thirty-four at Ternwatch asks whether the proof was made and is told that the yard has proved that the thing the offices say they are guarding against is a room, and has not proved what was in it. Cost: one mark and eightpence out.

### Chapter 300 — *Friday the Fourteenth of May, a Leaf With Four Columns Read Back, Thirteen Things That Are the Answer, and a Working Harbor Poorer on the Last Page Than on the First*

**Friday the fourteenth of May. The shed, and the mole steps in the afternoon.** **THE READ-BACK: thirty-two entries in the first column at the hour of it and thirty-three at the close, being twenty-three on the fourth of May, nine from the fifth to the thirteenth and one this evening; a hundred and twelve lines in the second at the hour of it and a hundred and fifteen at the close, being eighty-eight on the fourth, twenty-four in the nine days and three this afternoon; none in the third, with the boy's hand at the head of it and a name beside it that is not his and not the holder's; twenty-four in the fourth at the read-back and twenty-five at the close, the last of them the last thing in that book.** **Then thirteen things, read out in the flat voice she uses for a column, and entered without addition, and the fourth and the thirteenth are the two the yard is held to.** The fourth: **a seal on the last link of the old evidence chain, dated in the chain and older than the collar of the same office, reading *Sealed and entered at the Morrow circuit office by order of Magister Ilya Serr, senior Registry examiner*, and he is in no room.** The thirteenth: **a working harbor poorer on the fourteenth of May than on the twenty-third of February, and the four returns are four and there is no fifth, the five drawers are five drawers, the plate in eleven feet is in eleven feet with its note open, the forty marks stand twice, the wage column stands, and a yard has two hundred and sixty-one pence in a drawer on a mole.** And then the round of glass: **a person of no office goes up that coast on the seventeenth in a drawer in her own hand, and it will be on the eighteenth a thing in a house that this yard cannot reach, and her forty marks are not reduced and are not paid, and a man who has put a round of glass in a drawer in her hand has not bought a shilling of it. The round is a separate object and is NOT a piece of the plate that has closed — it is the collar-mounted disc that came off the floor of the room on the seventh and is bedded in a separate piece of worked glass, and the plate is still in the sill and went down into the rebate on the eleventh.** Cost: nothing in, nothing out. **THE BAG CLOSES AT ONE MARK, EIGHT SHILLINGS AND FIVEPENCE, BEING TWO HUNDRED AND SIXTY-ONE PENCE, and the figure the bag is short of the wage of four marks is three hundred and seventy-nine pence, being two marks, four shillings and eleven pence, and it is not going to be paid.**

### The batch's money, re-derived from the ten chapters and closing once

**THE BAG OPENS AT FIVE MARKS, ONE SHILLING AND FOURPENCE, BEING EIGHT HUNDRED AND SIXTEEN PENCE, AND CLOSES AT ONE MARK, EIGHT SHILLINGS AND FIVEPENCE, BEING TWO HUNDRED AND SIXTY-ONE PENCE. THE WHOLE OF THE BATCH IN ONE LINE: 816 + 649 − 1,204 = 261.** In: **six hundred and forty-nine pence on the fifth of May only**, being three marks for a day's work (480), a mark and eightpence for a boat and a man in her (168) and a penny of line (1) — **THE FIRST MONEY INTO THAT YARD SINCE THE SIXTH OF APRIL PAST, so the whole of what has come into it since the sixth of December past is now TWO THOUSAND FOUR HUNDRED AND NINE PENCE and not the one thousand seven hundred and sixty the four state files carried, and the figure of the eleven marks of Elias Voke on the sixth of April last is unchanged and is not superseded.** Out: **one thousand two hundred and four pence in four things** — two hundred and eight to a bonesetter on the eighth, six hundred and sixty to a glazier on the ninth, a mark and eightpence for a boat on the eleventh and a mark and eightpence for a boat on the thirteenth. **AND NOT ONE PENNY OF THE TWENTY-EIGHT MARKS, THREE SHILLINGS AND EIGHT PENCE OF PLANT AND AIR ON THE SIXTH AND THE SEVENTH WENT OUT OF THAT YARD AT ALL, because a man of sixty-one asked for the day at a pressure house at Ternwatch and the book sent the man and the house paid the man; that figure is entered in the first column and the fact that it did not touch the yard's own book is entered in the second, with the reason that the yard could not pay and has a rule about a man it has paid and none about a man paying for it.** **The whole of what has gone out of that yard since the sixth of December past is thirteen thousand nine hundred and five pence, being eighty-six marks, twelve shillings and one penny, in twenty-seven transactions at ten counters.** The wage of four marks is six hundred and forty pence: **the figure the bag is short of it had no subject from the seventh of April to the ninth of May, came back at forty-three pence on the ninth, and stands at three hundred and seventy-nine pence at the close, and it is not paid.**

### The batch's costs, in the order they were spent, and none of them paid twice

Six hundred and forty-nine pence of a day's work bought by a stranger; twenty-eight marks, six shillings and eightpence of plant and air on a man's name and not on the yard's; **an afternoon lost to the body on the sixth, with no figure because there is no figure for a day; a right hand of about forty in a man of sixty-one, a mark and four shillings, and an absence until the second of June; a boat of about nineteen tons in eleven pieces on the crown of a mole; a glass storm that cost the yard two lights and a skylight and cost the man of about fifty-five his own running glass and cost the man of about nineteen sixpence of his own; a permanent loss out of the holder's own recall with no figure for it; the only proof this yard will ever have of anything about a drowned city, gone into a plate of glass in a rebate; and a round of glass going up that coast in a drawer in a hand that is not the yard's.**
